madam president, mr president, do you really believe that your three promises of democracy, progress and peace can be fulfilled if europe does not become a chance for all? 137 million europeans live in rural areas. trust me, they don't just have to cross the street to get a job or get into a hospital. they have to travel long distances every day, tirelessly, and at a price, moreover, that keeps exploding. a significant proportion of these europeans have turned away from democracy by abstaining or, worse, by supporting the extremes. these citizens of the forgotten territories do not see europe as an opportunity to improve their lives and those of their families. they often see europe as an opportunity for others, those who do business, those who are hyper-connected or those who have done erasmus. not a single line, mr president, not a single word to tell them that the absolute priority of your presidency will be to offer them 5g, new technologies, or massive investment in public transport. not a word to the farmers either. every european citizen must be respected, regardless of his or her skin colour, beliefs, origin or geographical location. the real economic, social and democratic promise is that europe is a chance for all and for them too.
on behalf of the renew group. – mr president, the internet is a mirror; a mirror of the best and worst of our society. it mirrors the realities of our offline world. if the real offline world cannot be perfect, neither can the internet. but this does not mean that we should not try to make it a better and safer place, a place which respects freedom of speech while supporting our european businesses. this must be the goal of the digital services act. this is about creating a better system that will help us fight illegal content while giving citizens the right to challenge removals. it is about requiring the big giants to take a transparent look at themselves and to address the risks that are inherent within their systems. it is about making all providers, including marketplaces, understand that they have a social responsibility, and they must be honest and true in how they act towards us, their users. it is about protecting our children so they can avoid the worst of the internet. at the same time, we create a system that will support the growth of e—commerce and digital innovation in europe without unneeded over-regulation. we need start—ups, we need scale—ups in europe, and a system that works for them. today, we create a law and a justice system for the internet, a system that can work for the whole diverse internet. however, this law is just the first step. not the last one. we still have a lot of work ahead of us.

on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, the digital services act is a key legislation of this term, changing the functioning of the digital market across the entire european union. first of all i would like to thank the rapporteur, ms schaldemose, for her hard work done on this important file during the last few months. i would also like to express my support for the horizontal character of the text that we managed to maintain. my group was focused on preserving a robust and open digital economy in europe that proved its importance to citizens and businesses during the covid pandemic. this would not be possible without maintaining the key elements of the e—commerce directive, like the limited liability regime. in my capacity as the shadow rapporteur in the committee on the internal market and consumer protection, i mainly focus on strengthening the freedom-of-speech safeguards, on protecting users’ freedoms in the online world, and on removing the incentives that could cause hasty removal of content by platforms, which could consequently introduce soft internet censorship. indeed, the european conservatives and reformists support an open internet where users can move around freely, have content at their disposal and where their freedoms are respected. consequently, we closed loopholes in the text which would have encouraged the excessive removal of content, just in case there was a suspicion of its illegality. there was also a need to define when platforms should step in to tackle illegal content. there must be clarity so that users are preserved from the unilateral restriction of their freedom of expression. in this context, it is worth pointing out that we have succeeded in introducing some provisions to challenge platform discussions on content moderation. thanks to my group’s negotiations, the new tool will provide additional protection for users in the event of arbitrary decisions by websites. in addition to the platform’s internal complaints-handling system, users will be able to quickly and easily seek redress through the courts in their country of residence. it means that internet users will eventually gain access to robust tools, strengthening their position. however, despite a very good final text, i still believe it requires further improvements, especially regarding the introduction of social media definition, which has been included in my plenary amendments. member states cannot remain idle and inclusively rely on limited administrative capabilities, while very large social media platforms alter public discourse and arbitrarily influence the debate or are used for disinformation. i hope that during the trilogues with the council and the commission, together with members from other political groups, we will continue the dialogue and achieve a better digital environment for european citizens. we shall also remember that the digital market not only represents threats, but also great opportunities.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the the commission is not years of legislative inaction and regulatory inaction have led to the development of a digital surveillance capitalism driven by the profit interests of facebook, google and others. the digital services act, the very law that we're discussing here and now and today, is designed to put an end to this digital state of nature. consumers must be protected, and businesses must be given clear rules to follow and ensure that they are respected, and transparency must be ensured. but unfortunately the present draft falls far short of these expectations and requirements. there is progress, for example, in banning so-called dark patterns - those confusion tactics that trick users into agreeing to data mining. the obligation for large platforms to make accessibility for people with disabilities is also good news, as is the proposal that companies must make some of their data accessible. however, the draft has serious shortcomings. instead of finally ending the unscrupulous business with our most personal data, the digital services act unfortunately stands at this point after the first step. i am of course in favour of banning personalised advertising to minors. however, in order to effectively protect users, the extraction of highly sensitive data must be prohibited. the profits of facebook and other internet giants cannot be based on our political or sexual orientation and our health data. the generous derogations now being proposed for small and mediumsized enterprises create a monster of bureaucracy. transparency rules should instead apply to all companies with more than 10 employees. the whistleblower frances haugen had already pointed out to us here in the european parliament in november that the loopholes for companies must be closed. we cannot continue to allow platforms to hide behind the protection of trade secrets and to evade transparency rules. the game of hiding behind the country of origin principle by platforms like airbnb must be stopped. only by strengthening the legal capacity of member states and improving cooperation between authorities and platforms can illegal content and infringements be tackled consistently in the interest of tenants . in the run-up to the negotiations, i met and consulted with many ngos and other civil society actors. i do not know who others have met, but this text hardly reflects the wishes of civil society, but above all the positions and interests of the companies.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. it has been said many times that digital platforms have completely taken over our daily lives. in many cases they are also indispensable for some of our fellow citizens. but the cases of problems that have a truly catastrophic effect have been accumulating in recent years. and that is why it is late, ladies and gentlemen of the commission, but not too late for the european union to come to a decision of principle now with the law on digital services, which ensures once again that only what is right is allowed in europe. and the law in europe is made by the european parliament. and for that i would like to thank the rapporteur, but also our shadow rapporteur, arba kokalari, from the bottom of my heart. the fact that we are sitting here today after several months of hard negotiations and have found a compromise that certainly does not satisfy the epp group, but which also provides a good basis for negotiations with the council of ministers, is a great step forward, a great achievement of democracy. and for that, i want to thank you very much. i believe that the key achievement of the digital services act is that it brings order back to the digital single market. and that order has been destroyed in various places in the last few months. i just remember the news coverage, partly live coverage, of the terrorist attacks in new zealand. this is not something that should be on the internet. and with the digital services act, we're making sure that it can be monitored and banned in the future, as quickly as possible. secondly, ladies and gentlemen, we in the epp group have fought to preserve the principle of country of origin - not to hide behind it, but to ensure that innovative companies - and it has been pointed out - that young companies can quickly take advantage of the whole market in europe and are not held back by the old national borders. we want innovation and the whole single market to be available to everyone. thirdly, i would like to mention here, of course, the points on which we are far from satisfied, even in the case of such compromises, and where we believe that we still need to work on some points. with fake news, it is certainly true that the big media companies can do a lot to fight fake news. whether we have actually reached the best middle ground here remains open. there will be a lot of work to do on algorithm transparency to ensure that third parties actually get enough insight and that the authorities don't always chase after them. and so there are other points, mr president, which i cannot mention here. i would like to thank all my colleagues. i believe that this cross-cutting legislation is a major step forward in ensuring that the law is back in power in europe and that the gatekeeper is not.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the digital services act is indeed a way to write a new digital constitution for europe. we want to strengthen the fight against illegal content, we want to protect internet users and above all we want to overcome national isolation. we will hold platforms like google, facebook and amazon to a greater responsibility. and we're going to make sure, above all, that they're going to give access to the data, that researchers are going to have access to the algorithms. we're making sure there are no more black boxes, but more transparency. but there is certainly room for improvement in this proposal. for example, the present compromise does too little to prevent personalised advertising. this house voted in my own-initiative report about a year ago to ban personalised advertising. and i would like to call on all members to vote this time, as they did last year, to ban personalised advertising - there are all these amendments to that effect - or at least to ensure that we severely restrict such personalised advertising - there are also amendments to that effect. the negotiations were very intense and i would like to thank the negotiator, mrs schaldemose, very much for her persistence and for working so quickly. but there are indeed amendments now which are making the compromise dangerous and endangering fundamental rights. some meps from the epp, especially from the cdu, are now trying to reintroduce upload filters via stay-down orders in the last few metres. we have discussed upload filters with great passion here. no, mr schwab, this is not fake news. and just today, commissioner breton uploaded a video, a western, a clip from the good, the bad and the ugly. he couldn't have done that if this house hadn't made sure that parody was still possible and that upload filters didn't filter out that content. and now there's an amendment that would introduce exactly this upload filter for all platforms through the back door. that is really, really, really, really indecent, ladies and gentlemen. i'm equally shocked that the same group of mps want to make sure that dark patterns remain legal, meaning that users are being pressured into consent to use their data for advertising. the motion for a resolution is therefore for a separate vote. this is a joke on the citizens, it must not go through. so hopefully we can get this done, that these split votes don't go through, that these upload filters don't come through, and that we, with the dsa, create an internet that's fair for all users.
mr president, madam vice-president of the european commission, mr commissioner, two centuries later, the union is finally taking its responsibilities in the face of the digital revolution. yes, two centuries, ladies and gentlemen, since the last major text on e-commerce was written in the year 2000 and twenty years in digital terms, that is two centuries. digital is part of our lives and brings many benefits, opportunities, but also new threats to citizens and democracies. i'm talking about counterfeit products sold online, fake news, misinformation, hate speech, harassment, cyber threats, and so on, and so on and so on. with the dsa, an imperative: in europe, what is forbidden offline must also be online. it is the end of the wild west and i invite you to watch a video on twitter that the commissioner has just posted and which i think is very effective in this respect. i am convinced that the sdc will make europe an example for the rest of the world. in fact, in contrast to the chinese model where the state controls everything and the american model which advocates laissez-faire, we propose a balanced text which will ensure fair competition for european companies and better protection of citizens' rights. with this text, we are forcing the platforms to face reality and take all their responsibilities, a new system of sanctions will be put in place and, above all, we are opening the black box of the algorithms. we're introducing more transparency into the algorithms. this is unprecedented legislation worldwide and the union is a pioneer in this field. i think we should be proud of that. the dsa is a good basis for future negotiations. and as president emmanuel macron reminded us here this morning, the conclusion of these discussions under the french presidency is both possible and necessary. we must work towards that goal.
president, ladies and gentlemen, in our modern democracy, much of the social debate takes place on social media such as facebook. these digital platforms are modern information marketplaces. but there are no alternatives to this. they are monopolists, and that gives these companies great power, too great power. because they abuse that power. they control, block and censor anyone who disagrees with them. after all, their multicultural model should not be questioned, or they call it hate speech. their lies can't be refuted, or they call it fake news. they are tyrants for whom citizens no longer have rights but are degraded to submissive consumers, dependent on the arbitrariness of the anonymous manager and the interests of the rootless shareholder. free speech is fundamental to a democracy, just as censorship is fundamental to a dictatorship. that's why it's an inalienable right. it is not the big tech user agreement, but the constitution and our european treaties that establish and protect that right to free speech. that's why we need to stop the illegal censorship by big tech and prosecute. what's legal offline should be legal online. protecting free speech, not facilitating censorship, should therefore be the starting point of this digital services act.
mr president, there is a lot of good in the digital services act. there will be more transparency, better consumer protection and uniform rules for the digital single market. but i'm very concerned about freedom of speech, a fundamental right. any exception must be limited to this and it is unacceptable that a few powerful private players should be allowed to arbitrarily censor public debate, that blind algorithms should silence citizens. the major internet platforms are the mail and letter boxes of the 21st century and they must be subject to universal service obligations. everyone should have the right to post messages and receive messages. and the big platforms should only be allowed to remove well-defined, manifestly illegal content. any other discussion of free speech is a matter for the courts in a state governed by the rule of law. i come from a member state where the constitution prohibits censorship. let this be a guide for the european union.

in writing. the covid-19 pandemic has had devastating effects on the employment, education, training and mental well-being of young europeans. responding to the aspirations, employment and career prospects of those who are entering or have recently entered the labour market must be an eu priority. young people have suffered the most from job and income loss in those sectors where they are most present and have been most affected by the pandemic, such as retail, hospitality and tourism. the effects of school closures and social distancing on the psychological, social and educational dimensions of the most disadvantaged and vulnerable groups were equally important. the effectiveness of measures such as the 'enhanced youth guarantee' programme or initiatives such as alma will depend on the success of their proper implementation. much more needs to be done to enable young people to play a key role in shaping employment and social policies, to ensure quality learning and work opportunities, and to put an end to low wages and new forms of exploitation and discrimination.
the covid-19 pandemic has gripped our society for almost two years now. the impact on our young people is huge. first and foremost, there is the closure of educational institutions with potentially major implications for learning disability. but also the closure of sectors where many young people work. for example, youth unemployment increased from 11.9% in 2019 to 13.3% in 2020. restrictions on social activities have implications for the mental well-being of our young people. one in three said they had difficulty with the covid-19 measures. i am therefore very pleased that 2022 has been renamed the european year of youth. europe is making young people an absolute priority and is investing more than ever in the future of our continent. the youth guarantee has already been extended to support young people up to the age of 29 with a job, a new training course or a traineeship within four months of becoming unemployed or leaving school. at the request of parliament, additional attention will be given to young people with fewer opportunities or mental health problems. i therefore wholeheartedly support the 'alma' project proposed by ursula von der leyen in september to encourage international mobility among disadvantaged young people.
the rapporteur. madam president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, my aim here as rapporteur was to send a strong message to put an end to the transitional regime, which is a source of weaknesses and is increasing the vat gap. i want to thank the fictional reporters for their cooperation. this report was voted by a large majority in the econ committee, showing a united front to propose solutions to questions that have been outstanding for more than four years. this implementation report deals with particularly sensitive issues, which affect member states' policies, the efficiency of the internal market, the digital and environmental transition and the well-being of our citizens. the vat gap in the union has increased from 20% in 2009 to 10% in 2019, but it varies considerably between member states, which is detrimental to the union budget, businesses and european citizens. the first element is compounded by the large differences in standard rates between member states and the multitude of reduced and special rates. this complexity leads to opacity, compliance costs, errors and fraud. it severely limits the coherence and interoperability of the vat system and cost the union some eur 134 billion in 2019. this is why our report proposes several strategic options. we propose first to identify good practices at national level in order to apply them to a reform at european level. we also believe that a thorough examination of reduced and special rates is essential to reduce the cost of compliance and to modernise and rationalise the tax system in the interests of consistency. we also believe that while the pandemic has catalysed the development of digitalisation for all transactions, transparency, accountability and automated reporting still need to be ensured, which are essential for a definitive, simplified and sustainable vat regime. however, the commission must also support our smes in acquiring technology and know-how. in addition, our businesses urgently need the 'taxes in europe' database to be fully operational and to provide fast, up-to-date and accurate access to relevant information on the implementation of the vat system in the member states, in particular on all standard and lower vat rates. finally, we propose to support the climate transition by gradually removing harmful products, but also those that are highly polluting, from the list of derogations from the standard rates. such a phasing-out should include compensatory measures and reduced rates on basic necessities to help low-income households. on 7 december, ecofin ministers approved the long-awaited reform of the vat rate directive. the reform, as requested by parliament, provides for the phasing out of fossil fuels or products harmful to the environment and an acceleration of digitalisation. she doesn't want to leave anyone on the side of the road. it will therefore be up to the member states of the european union to ensure a fair balance between compliance with the standard rates and the derogations allowed, but also to avoid eroding the tax base when using the new reduced vat rates.
president, commissioner, rapporteur, dear véronique, after a year and a half of hard work, listening to experts and a working visit to the world health organization, we have sharpened our knowledge with progressive insight. the result is a comprehensive cancer plan. because the momentum is now. every year, 2.7 million europeans are diagnosed with cancer. here's a partner of a patient who's had cancer four times already. it puts you on an emotional rollercoaster, from the first bad news smoke to the autopilot in the treatments to a future perspective, at best from control to control. our objective was clear: a 360-degree approach that focuses on all stages of the disease and the patient. our proposals go beyond those of the commission, and that is good, that is the task of parliament. we ask for special attention to be paid to adolescents and young adults. they must be recognized as a distinct group. we are advocating a european right to be forgotten, better protection for our workers in the workplace, the joint purchase of medicines. every patient should get the best possible treatment. it can't be that your chances of survival depend on where you were born or what you earn. finally, as the president has already said, it cannot be more symbolic today. today is world childhood cancer day. let them be the benchmark for what we vote on tonight.
the honorable chairwoman. i thank the european parliament's special committee on cancer for the outstanding work and the final report we are voting on today. the vote for the european union is a vote for a common european fight against a disease that affects three million europeans a year at european union level. the vote for the united kingdom is a vote for closer european health union and a vote for more social justice and equality in healthcare, with less disparity in the survival rate of cancer patients still above 25%. i am well aware of the importance of timely diagnosis and the invaluable value of timely and quality treatment of cancer. this saved my life years ago and is why i support and advocate in all areas of my work for timely screening and detection of disease, for equal access for all europeans to the highest quality oncological care and to equally fast and affordable quality medicines. a vote for the resolution is a vote for life.

the commission has already made a number of proposals. cancer is a terrible disease that affects almost every family in europe. we are 10 per cent of the world's population in europe, but a quarter of all cancers diagnosed worldwide are in europe. i think cancer really needs to be one of our top priorities, but it's also a personal concern of mine that we're sending signals from here in parliament that we're doing everything as politicians that we can to fight cancer. three thoughts on this: first, i believe we need to invest heavily in research on prevention, diagnosis, treatment, follow-up care for the affected and their families. secondly, i think it's very important to invest in patient organisations as well. so what i see in my country is that patient organizations are making a very, very valuable contribution to cancer treatment, and we need to empower them. and third, i think banning certain lifestyles is not the way to go. i believe that a blanket ban on alcohol advertising at sporting events or a conspicuous warning against alcohol is the wrong way to go. we must of course combat alcohol abuse. but it is important that we do education work on the ground, especially with our children and young people, that we do prevention campaigns. i think that role models are much more effective and convincing for young people than we are here to issue bans.
madam president, i would like to thank you. so much brilliance has been said and so much available evidence is available about the fact that we can make a difference for so many people. each year, 120 000 european workers are exposed to dangerous substances and tens of thousands die. it is unacceptable that ordinary people should be put to death because of our inability to make reasonable demands on the employer. we need to ensure routines and raise the level of knowledge around the eu. it's obvious to me. it must not be because of social injustice and because of the inequalities that people in the eu have to put up with in trying to provide for themselves and their families in the hope of creating a better future. we can make great strides in this area and together we will succeed in fighting cancer. so let's all help each other find common solutions in the fight against cancer, so that no one has to die because of going to work.
madam president, please. the fight against cancer is not just for science, it's not just for society, it's not just for politics, it's for all of us. cancer kills more than 1.3 million people a year, including 6,000 children. 3 million new cases are diagnosed, and more than 12 million survivors struggle to return to normal life. it is important that the results of scientific research are communicated to everyone in a comprehensible way. there's a lot of misconceptions, unfounded, inaccurate information in the public consciousness. if we're afraid of beliefs, we won't even participate in our regular self-exams and screenings. therefore, it is very important to take active prevention measures by adopting a healthy lifestyle and regularly attending screening. it can't make a difference in curing cancer. the fight against cancer must be about people and the preservation of human dignity.
madam president, we are faced with a very comprehensive report on the characterization of this serious reality and the elements that the fight against cancer is currently shaping up to be. as in the pandemic situation, the role that national public health services have played and continue to play is essential. we are therefore not following the development of a so-called health union, decentralisation of powers or a privatising of health care. this requires the strengthening of free, high-quality national public health services, increased investment, more staff, more and better equipment, access to treatment and medicines, adequate follow-up at all stages of illness and family members, and the creation of a public care network to this end. the need to strengthen prevention policies and the role of the competent national authorities is apparent. it requires that, without prejudice to increased cooperation between states, each country maintain and develop its own capacities in the production of medicines and medical devices that allow the necessary response to cancer patients without being at the mercy of multinationals or dependent on supranational policies that restrict diversity and response capacity.
madam president, this report, which the special committee of which i am a member can be really pleased with, is a major milestone in the history of european cancer policies. it will help our governments to better strategies in the field of oncology, no doubt. if i had to summarize it, i would choose two concepts: prevention and equality. prevention is always the best health policy and oncology is no exception. to reduce the incidence of cancer in our societies requires transforming the habits and lifestyles as well as the social and environmental conditions of our population. the field we have to cover to achieve this goal is still immense. in this respect, our amendments have focused on the concept of health in all policies and we are pleased that they have been taken into account. the fight against inequalities between countries and regions in cancer treatment must be our undisputed priority as europeans. there are still unbearable differences between our citizens in terms of screening, early detection, overtreatment and therapeutic innovations. in conclusion, i would like to point out that we can understand that the genetic code determines the probability of survival in the face of a cancer diagnosis, but what we cannot accept, in any way, is that this probability depends on the postal code.
madam president, 1.3 million people die of cancer in the eu every year. every nine seconds, a new case of cancer is diagnosed in the eu, meaning that by the time i’ve finished speaking, six people will have been diagnosed. this is already shocking, but studies show that it will only get worse, that by 2035, the number of cases could double and an estimated 40% of the population will face cancer at some point in their lives. covid has showed us that we can and should do more at european level to save lives. we need to invest more in research to utilise new technologies like ai, and to ensure earlier detection and better care, and we need to share best practices. the fight against cancer is not new. we have been pledging to defeat cancer for a long time. i don’t know if we will ever be truly free of it, but i know that, as the european union, we can do more. so let’s do it.

in writing. the cancer report is a very thorough strategy document, preceded by a huge amount of analysis. it describes the effects of air pollution, alcohol, tobacco, radon and other components on cancer, as well as the deadly effect of covid-19 on cancer. tobacco products are considered the biggest cause of preventable cancer and therefore i support the target to reduce tobacco consumption from the current twenty-five per cent to five per cent by 2040 in the fight against cancer. an honest analysis will help to reduce the number of cancer deaths in europe.
in writing. the proposed text contains important ideas in the fight against cancer. one sentence particularly struck me: strengthening public health services, both in terms of funding, infrastructure and health professionals, the report says, is essential to improve cancer prevention, screening and detection. i did! but in so doing the report rejects the whole policy of austerity pursued by the commission and the european governments. austerity has brought health care systems to their knees. how can effective prevention campaigns be carried out with a public apnea system? how can you get an early diagnosis when you're faced with endless waiting lists? or what about the delay in care during the covid-19 pandemic because our hospitals were underfunded and understaffed? under-investment, lack of infrastructure, unbearable working conditions for workers and prohibitive cost for the patient... the effective fight against cancer requires a break with european policies. we need massive investment in public health care systems. making the fight against cancer a priority means breaking with the commodification and commercialization of health.
president, 60 years, 60 years of development aid shows that it doesn't work, except for those who really don't want to see it. i was ten years old when the french came to my village school in burkina faso. they gave us pencils and books, but we already had them. "because we were poor", they said, "they came to help us". after class, i ran home sobbing. dad, we're poor, did you know that? "don't worry, child", he said. it's okay to be poor. i knew we weren't rich, of course, but i never thought we'd be so helpless. i felt like i was suddenly deprived of my wings. as a teenager, i saw european leaders in the spotlight in africa announcing huge checks. we wondered where all that money would go, what would change for us except that africa would go deeper into debt. the last check i saw on tv was from president chirac. when i see commission president von der leyen in dakar now, more than 20 years later, waving a cheque, i have a feeling of déjà vu. more pens and pencils. the nanny diplomacy is alive. this breaks my heart and makes me furious as a legislator. that signature, the gesture, the eternal lie, a hug that chokes. please stop the nanny diplomacy in africa! that must be the mandate of the eu-africa summit. save the european citizen the money and the african youth the humiliation. in burkina faso, i see no development, but i do see a russian flag flying and jihadists triumphing. what did chirac's check bring in? he made people lazy. i ask macron and call on him to leave his checkbook at home on thursday!

the single market is the main source of prosperity in the eu. the lack of access to the single market would reduce my country's gross national product by as much as 18%. the report rightly calls for the completion of the single market. recent studies have shown that this would increase our combined national product by nine percent. so the profits are there. unfortunately, protectionism is on the rise and more and more retailers are selling only products from their own country. this forces companies to relocate their production and undermines confidence in the european union. these obstacles in fact are therefore rightly mentioned in the report. i therefore call on the commission once again to take a strong stand against this.

the institutions of the european union are increasingly appropriating powers which no one has ever given them and for which they are accountable to no one. the european commission was originally set up as a body of officials to carry out what the member states agree, but it is increasingly behaving as a kind of european government that wants to dictate policy in the member states. citizens are especially wondering who these people are who have not been elected by anyone. technocracy is the opposite of democracy. the european court of justice was originally set up to review the application of the european treaties. by a broad and activist interpretation of the treaties, the court places itself above the constitutions of the member states in areas where it does not even have jurisdiction. it gives itself the power to assess the internal organisation of member states, to make policy choices and even to rewrite laws against which there is no appeal. the european parliament has also been asked to give its opinion on the draft budget for the european union for the financial year 2000. jurisdiction is the opposite of democracy. the commission and the court then work together to deprive member states who do not want to bow to their liberal sharia law of the right to vote and to blackmail them financially by withholding billions from the corona recovery fund. it's now clear that this was never about corona, but about power and control. this seizure of power is then sold as a defence of the democratic rule of law. it really can't get any more hypocritical than this. the rule of law can only be democratic if it respects the sovereignty of the people, and thus democracy. the organization and political choices of states should not be determined by the judges and officials of the european union, but by the nation states themselves. they are democratically legitimized by their people. the european union is not. the european union is completely losing its mr president, i would like to thank you. climate policy must have a gender perspective. an effective climate policy in sweden, in the eu, globally should require women to be at the negotiating table. women's influence on the policy and technical solutions to be prioritised. if we take a gender perspective, we also see very clearly how climate is linked to a justice perspective. we are seeing in concrete terms how we are creating societies that are resilient and sustainable, both socially and ecologically. on the left, we have a program that puts these pieces together. we see the need to protect nature and adopt ambitious climate targets. we see that we have to reset our production, our industry, if we're serious about actually dealing with the climate. we see the need to invest in care, in welfare, in what holds society together, where women are also the majority of those who work. we see that we need to allocate resources in a way that is much more equitable, and where those who pollute the most have to contribute more to the transition. it is, my friends, a feminist, red and green climate change.
mr. chairman, please! gender equality is one of the fundamental values of the european union. i think it's safe to say that we're a leader in promoting this idea, which is to make sure that we're working for security, peace, economic growth, environmental improvement. there is no doubt that there are links between gender and climate change and environmental challenges. all the research and data shows that it is mainly women and children who are the victims of conflict, climate change and disputes over natural resources. on the other hand, the full and equal participation of women in political and financial decision-making, including those related to sustainable development and the fight against climate change, has a significant impact on the positive effects of the actions undertaken. and while women are increasingly influencing economic change, entrenched social attitudes are significantly slowing it down. in many places, women's rights are being challenged, access to the labor market, education, health care is being restricted. violence against women is common, including sexual violence, which has been further exacerbated by the covid pandemic. but i hope that the strategies and programs that are being implemented, i hope that the attitudes of the younger generation, i hope that the activism of many ngos, which i would like to thank from this place, and i hope that the responsibility of wise politicians will raise social awareness and contribute to solving the problem of inequality and support positive change and transformation.
mr president, dear commissioner dalli, dear secretary of state, climate emergency is our reality, as well as gender inequality and both are intrinsically linked. but so is the solution. when we take gender—responsive climate action, we further close gender inequalities. when we have more women in decision—making, we have better, more effective decisions also on climate mitigation and when we empower women, especially in indigenous communities, we protect mother nature. so yet, let us not forget europe claims to be a leader in both fields. but we still are far from reaching equality and climate neutrality. the upcoming un session on the status of women in march is the place where we can show the commitment, but also take the necessary action.
the commission's proposal is to be adopted by the end of the year. it is a matter for our society, it is a matter for culture, but it is first and foremost a matter for the european union itself. the founding declaration of the european union, which is the 'constitution' of the european union, stated that without any concessions, delays or shortcomings, the european union was to implement gender equality. i am a proud european because i regard the european union as the best, the greatest, the strongest, the most important achievement of mankind. however, the failure to achieve this objective, laid down in the treaties of the european union, is the great gap, not of the sexes, but of the declarations of the european union with the acts. it is not possible for european citizens to have waited 65 years for gender equality to be implemented, as required by the treaty of rome of 25 march 1957, and to have only made beautiful declarations, speeches and appeals, while gender equality has not been achieved. the numbers are staggering. what we expect here in the european parliament, and what 500 million citizens expect, is that the european commission and the european council come to the next meeting and tell us that gender equality has been achieved, or at least that the european commission and the european council commit themselves to sanctions against those member states which do not implement gender equality. but when even the european institutions, bodies and agencies of the european union themselves do not implement gender equality, how can we expect it to be implemented in all the countries of the european union? i am putting my proposal forward and i expect you, commissioner, to implement it.

in writing. the 66th session of the un commission on the status of women provides an opportunity for global discussion to identify challenges and formulate commitments to be implemented in promoting gender equality and women's empowerment in the context of climate change and environmental disasters. these phenomena are a constant threat to the peoples of the planet and have a disproportionate impact on women, in particular those in situations of vulnerability and marginalization, through inequalities in access to essential resources and services, poverty, conflict, violence, discrimination and migration. actions towards climate neutrality and fossil energy independence will need to take into account specific gender needs, so that the opportunities emerging in the green transition are equally beneficial and accessible. a renewed commitment to the fight to remove the structural and cultural barriers that generate unequal participation of women in decision-making, labour markets and pay gaps will provide equal opportunities to contribute to a sustainable and inclusive economy, and to the prosperity and well-being of societies.
according to un figures, an average of 21.5 million people have been displaced annually since 2010 as a result of natural disasters arising from climate change. as many as 80% of the displaced are women and girls. it is urgent that this issue be brought to the attention of the annual session of the un commission on the status of women. climate change is affecting women proportionately more. in addition, women are still underrepresented in national decision-making bodies on climate change, and represent only 32% of the renewable energy workforce worldwide. it is high time we came up with gender-responsive measures to address displacement, poverty, human trafficking, gender-related violence and food insecurity. this is why we need to focus more than ever on gender-targeted initiatives such as gender mainstreaming in the european green deal. finally, we need to make extra efforts to ensure that women play a more prominent role in climate policy.

and intelligent traffic management are vital for safety, to avoid congestion and to achieve the emission reduction targets we have set ourselves. the credibility and fairness of this system requires on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, we are discussing again a matter which is related to a new regulation. regulation which should create new conditions and new pressures for people working for small and mediumsized businesses. again, all of you here, especially the 10 people in the room, as you can see, we want to make new regulations and make these people spend more money. this is not right. we're in the group where we think there should be less regulation, and we think we should let people work, make money. we think that's much better. we think it's much more fair.
on behalf of the left group. mr president, the european union may well try to paint its policies green and claim marked reductions in environmental impacts. it cannot hide the set of interests that serve the processes of liberalization of sectors at the expense of the interests of peoples, of labour and of workers' rights, compromising the provision of services, development and the territorial, social and economic cohesion of states. it is the large economic and financial groups which, by determining and benefiting from these policies, rub their hands with the prospects of profit far removed from any concern for the environment. the so-called eurovignette directive, which we are discussing here, is a modest example of this. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the road transport sector is not subject to the same tax burden as other sectors. the impact of road transport on greenhouse gas emissions is undeniable. however, what this directive proposes is to contribute to the expansion of road network charges, putting a burden on the general public, who often have no alternative, either for the transport of goods or for their mobility options. the proposal directly interferes with a national competence: taxation policy, by transferring revenues from the states to the concessionaires of the road networks. it also incorporates an unacceptable dimension subject to the perverse system of emissions trading. this legislation will impose increases in fares and travel, penalising those who have no options in the face of poor public transport or freight supply. development, cohesion and mobility are affected. you don't do what's necessary.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, unfortunately there are some flaws in this text. some member states today - particularly belgium - have the unfortunate idea - fortunately not yet implemented - of introducing a mileage tax on private cars, which would obviously have important consequences for the employment of people living in rural areas. the text will not prevent this kind of bad thinking. secondly, the revenue earmarked for this additional burden is not automatically and necessarily directed towards investments in new infrastructure, multimodal transport and relief for certain regions. there is no obligation in this respect in what is presented and proposed in this text. various amendments are attempting to improve the situation. i hope, of course, that these amendments will be supported, particularly in order to provide solutions for the allocation of revenue and to give this impetus to multimodal transport, particularly rail transport, and also to relieve certain regions, simply by the reality they are experiencing today. for these reasons, at this stage - and it will obviously depend on the development of the debate - it is not possible, at least for me, to support this text as it stands. i hope that various amendments will be adopted to improve it in the sense i have just indicated.
the iberian peninsula is experiencing one of the worst winter droughts in decades. reserves of water in spain and portugal are facing their worst figures in years and there are no forecasts of rain in the coming weeks. between 1 october and 1 february, rainfall in spain was 36 per cent less than the 1981-2010 measure. climate change experts warn that warming will lead to more evapotranspiration in the mediterranean region and that the available water reserves will be smaller, with situations like the current one recurring more frequently. in addition, the risk of fires will be higher and the harvests will suffer greater impacts. the socialists in this parliament are in favour of the need for eu support, cooperation between member states, efficient and rational management of water resources, the albufera convention and a european strategy on desertification. we also work to ensure that the most vulnerable and poorest populations are not affected any more and we oppose the unfeasible absorption target assigned to spain in the new proposal for the utcuts regulation, which does not take into account the impacts of climate change.
the next item is the report by mrs stefania zambelli, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs, on the proposal for a directive of the european parliament and of the council amending directive 2004/37/ec on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens or mutagens at work (com ((2020) 0571 c9-0301/2020 2020/0262 cod)) (a9-0114/2021).
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, fighting against cancer is one of the key priorities of the commission and, i know very strongly, of your parliament. it has to be a priority because today 52% of annual occupational deaths in the eu can be attributed to work—related cancers. these figures show the importance of the file we are discussing today. improving workers’ health and safety in the context of rapidly changing economic, technological and work patterns. this fourth update of the carcinogens and mutagens directive revises the limit values for three key carcinogens widely used in the eu, and we estimate that it will improve the protection of more than one million workers in the eu. and i’m very pleased to see that both the european parliament and the council supported these limit values throughout the discussions. but we also went much further in this update, and i thank the parliament and particularly the rapporteur, and i wish you all well, having listened to your personal experience, as well as the former president of the committee on employment and social affairs for the very ambitious approach of the parliament. firstly, the co—legislators agreed to include the reprotoxic substances in the scope of the directive. we will now be working further on putting in motion this amendment. another substantial point of discussion was the protection of workers, including nurses, from the exposure to hazardous medicinal products. we all agree that as soon as these medicinal products have carcinogenic, mutagenic and reprotoxic properties, they fall within the scope of the carcinogens and mutagens directive. however – and i must say, i appreciate that very much – your negotiators in the trilogue strongly defended the need to ensure a higher degree of clarity on this specific aspect, and for that reason, both the co—legislators and the commission agreed on making statements to clarify this. the provisional agreement includes reinforced training measures and, among other elements, the publication of guidelines, and i am strongly committed to publish these guidelines by the end of this year. we have to better protect those who every day take care of those patients who need these treatments. but we know now better than ever, the crucial role of health workers – and it has been mentioned by the rapporteur – who every day take risks to bring health to those who suffer from cancer, and it is not acceptable that they have to take these risks. so, these guidelines will be clear and they have not just to be there, they have to be implemented and their implementation also has to be controlled. the third substantial point in this discussion, was the introduction of a new methodology for setting limit values at the eu level, a so—called risk—based methodology. on this issue, we all realised that we will still need time to assess the relevance of such an approach, and we will continue our work on this topic. this provisional agreement also provides that the commission reviews the limit value for respirable crystalline silica and adopts an action plan this year for proposing, where appropriate, new or updates of limit values for an additional 25 substances. and as i mentioned already, this is already the fourth revision of the carcinogens and mutagens directive, and since 2016, the commission has submitted proposals to address 30 chemicals. in addition, as announced in the new eu strategic framework on health and safety at work, we have already planned the fifth revision addressing five additional chemicals and therefore such an action plan is fully in line with the commission’s desire to constantly improve the protection of workers against occupational cancer. mr president, honourable members, before concluding, i would like to highlight the key and constructive role again played by both the european parliament and the council because this proposal was adopted and approved at the council level by unanimity, which shows that everybody now is aware of the importance of what we are doing here. the provisional agreement we have before us today is ambitious and it is another significant step forward in improving the health of eu workers. and it shows also that europe cares about the health of its citizens and workers. and, therefore, i am confident that you, members of the european parliament, will largely support this text so that it will turn into practical measures on the ground very quickly.
the commission is therefore proposing to amend the proposal to the effect that the commission should be able to take part in the negotiations. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, cancer, whether of occupational origin or not, is the second leading cause of death in the european union, after cardiovascular disease. they are responsible for about a quarter of the total number of deaths. they also put national health and social care systems, public budgets, productivity and economic growth to the test. for all these reasons, the fight against cancer must be strengthened. there is a political consensus to support this objective. it is in this context that we are today undertaking to improve the protection of workers against the risks associated with exposure to carcinogens or mutagens at work. cancer is responsible for 52% of all occupational deaths each year, and that is far too many. to achieve this improvement, it is necessary to amend directive 2004/37/ec, which is the basic text for the protection of workers against cancer. the committee on legal affairs voted 15 amendments to add to the list. one of the main ones recommends, for the sake of transparency, that medical prescriptions should only be drawn up after consultation with the economic and social actors concerned. another suggests that national and european stimulus plans should be used to finance research programmes on the health risks to workers. a third pointed to the particular case of workers exposed to a cocktail of dangerous substances, which would require a downward revision of the limit values to take account of the combined effects of these products. a fourth point is that cobalt is carcinogenic and should therefore be subject to specific limit values. other amendments specify deadlines, including a deadline of at least every five years for reviewing limit values. i shall conclude with two more general considerations. the committee on legal affairs would draw your attention in particular to the fact that smes and microenterprises have limited financial, technical and human resources. care must therefore be taken that the application of the directive does not have disproportionate consequences for them. the committee would also like to point out that the directive does not prevent member states from providing for even more protective provisions if they so wish. in conclusion, the fight against cancer is a major european cause, to which the committee on legal affairs is happy to contribute.
the european parliament has been asked to vote on the report by mr glinne, on behalf of the group of the european people's party. we approved yesterday the european plan for the fight against cancer and today we are voting on the fourth revision of the directive on the protection of workers from the risks of exposure to carcinogens or mutagens at work. almost a year and a half after the european commission's proposal for a directive, the european parliament can present a fantastic result. this review is a real milestone in the fight against cancer due to occupational exposure to dangerous substances. the directive already included limit values for occupational exposure to 30 carcinogens and mutagens and is now being extended to include substances harmful to reproduction and dangerous medicinal products. the european parliament has been working hard to achieve this since the first revision of the directive. but our work is not over. in the european union, 80 000 workers still die of cancer every year as a result of occupational exposure to the substances mentioned above. this must be stopped by 2022. the fourth revision of the directive should therefore be followed up by the commission as soon as possible with an action plan to address the next 25 substances on the list of dangerous carcinogens, mutagens and reproductive toxicants. to achieve this, both the recommendations of the report of the special committee on cancer, on which we voted yesterday, and those of the report on the protection of workers from asbestos, which was put to the vote in october 2021, need to be transposed into legislation. only in this way can we ensure that workers no longer die of cancer simply because they are doing their jobs. i therefore expect that this report will receive a great deal of support.

member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, i am grateful for the opportunity we had today to discuss once more the eighth environmental action programme (eap), and i would like to thank all of you for your interventions. our citizens, in particular the youngest, expect us to address the climate and environmental crisis. yes, in the meantime, tragically, we are facing a new crisis caused by russia’s war on ukraine, which is threatening peace and stability in europe and beyond. it focuses our energy and attention, and rightly so. but the immense threat caused by climate change and by the destruction of our ecosystems remain and the war and its impact make our fight against this crisis even more relevant and more urgent than before. with the eighth eap, we have a joint strategy to 2030 and for the green transition. its strong narrative will focus minds and get everyone on board. it’s a plan for regional and local authorities, for citizens and businesses, for entrepreneurs, scientists and researchers. and thank you for recalling today the key challenges ahead. the need to review our tax system, the commitment to move to a well-being economy and environmental governance are all extremely important. let me briefly address some of the key points raised in today’s debate. concerning the next steps on environmentally harmful subsidies. i can say that we have a good plan, but very challenging circumstances. the eighth eap supports the commission’s effort to take ambitious action on phasing out environmentally harmful subsidies as soon as possible. we have to be clear that more subsidies for fossil fuels means less funds for other priorities – social policies, job creation, improving quality of life, including by better environment and less pollution. it is a fallacy that fossil fuel subsidies benefit the most vulnerable members of our society. there are other ways to help them. at the same time, those people are most exposed to environmental degradation and pollution. for fossil fuel subsidies, we will continue on the path set out in the governance regulation and the climate law. the commission is preparing an implementing act for the integrated national energy and climate progress report, which member states will have to submit every two years starting from 15 march 2023. the eap plan for adoption this year will also include a methodology for reporting on the phase out of energy subsidies, in particular for fossil fuels. for non-fossil fuel subsidies, there will be a sequencing of steps, notably on monitoring, reporting and a toolbox for phasing them out. let me now say just a few words on the focus on well-being. the beyond gdp narrative is very important to me. we need to ensure that we have a comprehensive approach to people, planet and prosperity. beyond gdp does not mean we do not care about people’s income, about energy or other forms of poverty. it’s just the opposite. the beyond gdp narrative and the commission has developed many new indicators in recent years to measure progress, especially on well-being and planetary boundaries, and the sustainable development goals are now being mainstreamed in all policy cycles, including the upcoming european semester spring package. now we are working also on finalising the monitoring framework for the eighth eap. once this is done, i will further pursue the eap call for measuring economic performance and societal progress beyond gdp and moving towards using well-being as a compass for policy. honourable members, dear colleagues, thank you once again for your support, for your relentless work and for your commitment to achieve this very good result.
the rapporteur. i would like to thank the president-in-office. for over a hundred years, european workers have been fighting for a better working environment. yet over 3 300 people die at work in the working conditions as a light in the dark - a path to a better working environment for all. we have negotiated a great report in the house. thank you to my colleagues for the incredible work we've
