threatened. the worm is in the fruit. no, this is not a purely national issue: european law has been violated. citizens all over europe have been spied on illegally, sometimes by their own government, sometimes by another state. the integrity and security of the european union, its institutions and its representatives are at stake. the commission and the council, which i regret not being with us at the moment, at the time of this debate, have already been more prompt to take swift action on security matters. i want to hear you strongly condemn these acts of espionage. i want to hear you say that investigations are under way and that the breach of parliamentary immunity is a red line. no, the hearing of parliamentarians, political opponents, journalists, lawyers or human rights defenders is not a matter of national security only, in a europe driven by democratic values, the rule of law and respect for fundamental rights. on the occasion of the international day of press freedom yesterday, journalists denounced a new normal which they must take into account: a new normal, where this type of espionage in the exercise of their profession is legion. well, no, this kind of espionage can't be a new reality that we have to deal with. we have to legislate. the response to threats to our security must be strong: trade sanctions, revision of judicial and police cooperation agreements, strict enforcement of the eprivacy regulation and the dual-use regulation, the due diligence directive for european companies, and so on. the tools are at our disposal, but they are nothing without a very strong political will. i expect all political groups to lift the taboos. our inquiry committee will have to provide the answers you do not give. it will have to establish responsibility, provide victims with remedies and reparations, ensure that sanctions are taken against offenders and lead to bans of these tools. the debate today is important. we expect parliament, through the voice of our president, to be firm in protecting its own members, to recall the principle of parliamentary immunity and to condemn the spying on its members. and the message must be clear: this type of interference is unacceptable and will not be tolerated. our house must ensure the safety of all its members and also its staff. it should be noted that some of the participants were also targeted by these wiretaps. an attack on one of us is an attack on all of us. in addition to setting up our committee of inquiry, may this parliament make a strong gesture by calling on the european commission and the council to clearly denounce the illegal use of this type of spyware, to open investigations and ensure transparency, but also to cooperate as much as possible with our committee of inquiry. because, i repeat, it is not just parliamentarians who are concerned, but all citizens and the european institutions.
mr. chairman, can you imagine? you sit at home and strangers come and sit with you. they're watching you, they're listening to all your conversations at all times. that's how pegasus works, i read. i find that very scary, creepy. for months, we've seen one pegasus revelation after another pass by. government leaders, journalists and activists: no one seems safe. at the same time, we know very little. - by who? to what end? what are the consequences? these questions are still barely answered. the pegasus spy software also asks us a different kind of question: how can we use technology to reconcile the need for knowledge and information with the vulnerability of our projects and freedoms? freedom of the press is something we must cherish and protect. the elected representatives of the people must be able to exercise their democratic mandate freely. but technology can indeed be used for freedom. it then needs democratic control and embedded in the rule of law. we also want to answer these questions in the committee of inquiry, to which i will be fully committed as a member. all i know is i don't know anything. that's what the greek philosopher socrates would have said. by asking questions, looking at the facts, and thinking, one comes to wisdom. that's how we work.

mr. president, i'd like to begin by quoting hillary clinton: women are always the first victims of war. they lose their husbands, their fathers, their sons in battle. and i'll add something to this: they also often lose their dignity. and the women's council is spelling it correctly. armed conflict is no longer a matter of armies fighting far from the civilian population. women and children account for as much as 70% of civilian casualties. this is no coincidence. rape and other sexual barbarity are systematically used as weapons of war. in addition, millions of ukrainian women and children have also fled to europe. both inside and outside the conflict zone, they are at increased risk of falling into the hands of rogue individuals and organisations. to protect their human dignity, we need to move much faster, including an action plan to prevent and combat crime, more specialized health and trauma care for abused women and girls, a coordinated approach to unaccompanied minors, and special attention to the brave women in the ukrainian military who must survive in captivity today. we demand strict supervision of compliance with the international conventions on the law of war. and it was our parliament president roberta metsola who was the first leader to visit ukraine. perhaps it is time and i agree that we take un resolution 1325 out of the dust, so that more women are involved in peace negotiations. often they turn out to be the game changers in gruesome wars.
in writing. when an agreement was reached on a global minimum rate of 15% for multinationals, it was undeniably a breakthrough in the fight against tax havens and tax avoidance by large corporations. it should be remembered that the european union had defended a rate of just 12.5% at the g7. what a scandal! europe has truly acted as an ambassador for big business. even the us government - not exactly a nest of leftists - had proposed a 21% global rate. the end result is far from perfect. this 15% rate is far below the corporate tax rate in many european countries. yet since the neoliberal turn of the 1980s, this rate has already been eroded by neoliberal policies across europe. in belgium, the rate was previously 48%. for the highest profits, it was even over 50%. today, many european countries, including belgium, apply a rate of just 25%. but that is still far more than the proposed 15%. in addition, all tax niches allow multinationals across europe to circumvent, avoid or even escape tax. there's still work to be done. tax the rich.

mr president, every time the european parliament meets, we talk about human rights, we vote resolutions, often with sanctions. russia, of course, china, belarus, and so on. but what strikes me is that since i have been a member of the european parliament, we have not voted a single resolution condemning israel. not once a resolution for sanctions against israel. even now, after the murder of that palestinian journalist, the attack on the funeral, the seizure of palestinian homes by israeli settlers, the imprisonment of children: even then, no traditional group is calling for sanctions against israel. that's taboo around here, dealing with israel. this is a very serious matter, and i am sure that the european union will not be able to accept it. that is totally unbelievable, because then we say that if a country does not do what we want, then we will complain about human rights, about democracy. but if a country does what we want, if a country follows us blindly and serves our geopolitical interests, then we remain silent and that country can do anything it wants. that's not defending human rights, that's abusing human rights for our own geopolitical agenda. and that is unacceptable.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, i have asked for this debate because i want to get an answer to a very important question, and that is the following. it's to the commission, and i want a straight answer: yes or no. are there any countries, public and private companies in the european union that buy and pay for russian gas under the scheme that gazprom has proposed? that's all i want to hear from you, nothing else. - yes or no? is there or isn't there? because if there is, it will be a whirlwind of hypocrisy and duplicity in the european commission, the union and parliament once again. while we're talking about sanctions and how we should fight the bad guys and fight them, someone is making a separate agreement because he's more concerned about his economy and doesn't care about anyone else. gazprom has categorically violated the contract with bulgaria, which was absolutely legal. he violated it illegally with bulgaria and poland, and stopped the shipment of already paid quantity. yes, it's an addiction and it's aggression, but it's solved by common action, and it's not solved when someone separately buys gas and pays in rubles. that's what i want you to answer me for. and to you, colleagues, a final conclusion. this crisis has taught us this. it is time to end the utopias, the ideologizing of the so-called green deal that drives up prices. because you, in this room, are raising prices when there is a constant push for utopian crazy ideas to reduce the production of electricity from normal sources. and what we need to do is turn to nuclear energy, to our own resources, to conventional gas, to shale gas, everything we have to have a normal economy, and not to be dependent on hostile third countries, on the one hand, or green utopias.

mr president, executive vice-president timmermans, dear chrysoula, it is an imminent need to speed up the transformation of our economy in the eu. it is also the time to seriously enhance our strategic autonomy. at this period of time we need also to provide to the market the proper stability and predictability with the legislation at stake. in that sense, starting with ets, on which the rapporteur peter liese did a great job, it is important to underline that now we make a significant step forward by having the maritime sector on board with particular funding and support to achieve innovation and a willing transformation in the next period of time. as far as the social climate fund is concerned, i fully support esther de lange and david casa’s proposal to support the population in need without adding burden to the households as the energy crisis is up and running. regarding to cbam, i would like to be clear: we need this too. we need this mechanism in relation to the carbon prices without jeopardising the competitiveness of the sectors involved. for that reason, i fully support the establishment of cbam, as well as realistic implementation when it comes to the phase—out of free allocations, combined with realistic solutions for ecs. dear colleagues, tomorrow we vote and we have to take the responsibilities. our own ones. dear colleagues, the fit for 55 package is an updated, more coherent package that sticks to the von der leyen coalition. we need to show to our citizens who struggle today with the energy crisis that we send a clear message to all these who are abiding with eu values and that we bring solutions to raised problems while we stand united toward the achievement of europe’s strategic objectives.
mr president, mr vice-president, madam minister, ensuring fair, smart and effective decarbonisation is an existential challenge for humanity, for the european union and for all of us. the choice we face is a structural one and will shape our collective future for many decades to come. it is an opportunity for the european union to strengthen its commitment to combating climate change by ensuring, through concrete and wide-ranging measures, a reduction of greenhouse gas emissions of at least 55% by 2030. the integrated and progressive implementation of these measures is also a way to boost the modernisation of european society and the economy and to consolidate the union's leadership in the energy and digital transition. as the socialists and democrats' shadow rapporteur on the negotiation of the itre border carbon adjustment mechanism, i consider it essential that this mechanism fulfils its role of promoting and supporting the decarbonisation agenda of european industry, while encouraging transformation and innovation dynamics that lead it to higher levels of sustainability and competitiveness. it will also encourage the adoption of good environmental practices in other parts of the world, thus contributing to a positive partnership on behalf of common objectives. these are not easy goals, but they are possible. this requires, in addition to a reasonable timetable for implementation and selective support for exports where justified and in line with wto law, strong monitoring and ongoing alignment of measures with the technological maturity necessary to make them viable. we have room to approve solutions in this respect and that is what we must now focus on.
a uachtaráin, the climate law is set in stone: the end point of the green transition – 2050 for climate neutrality. we have the finish line and tomorrow we get the opportunity to decide, in part, how we run the race. europe is and should be a climate leader. however, it is a fine balance. we want and need more ambition in decarbonising our economy, but not at the expense of severe loss of competitiveness. if we overburden ourselves, that will be a net negative consequence for the climate, as we will have less ability to pay for the technologies needed for the transition. the war has changed everything, and we have rightly accelerated our actions. investment in renewables stays in europe, but we need to keep a holistic approach and ensure we have the capacity. this means more emphasis on skills, especially in the building renovation sector, clear regulatory signals, including fixing the problematic planning and permitting procedures so we can get green technologies actually deployed. and of course the proper investment. in essence, the transition only comes with good timing and appropriate incentives. i fully support the targets for 2030 to transition to climate neutrality, and i believe this can only be successfully achieved if based on pragmatism.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. you have told us well here today that we want more jobs in industry. i think that is a great start, because we need europe to work and we need a sustainable transition to a climate neutral europe. the whole package will be voted on in this parliament tomorrow, but it is already clear that there will be strong support for the continuation of the ets. we need a price on coal. we need investment security for new industrial investment in europe and we can do this because we are finally getting the carbon levy mechanism in place. the coal mechanism will no longer allow europe to turn a blind eye to the fact that we use steel produced elsewhere and do not look at its emissions here. however, i am concerned that the cbam coal mechanism has not yet provided a solution for exports. parliament is also coming up with export-related solutions. i want to encourage parliament's negotiators to the final outcome, whatever form it takes in the votes. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'closed loop' to cover the whole of the european union. i wish you a successful negotiation.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is an open door policy to say that we are going to vote on a historic package of measures this week. to continue on the path to a climate-neutral continent, we must be ambitious and social. a more comprehensive emissions trading system, a carbon tax on our european external border and climate neutral vehicles. and in belgium we are setting our ambition for the latter even higher. but, ladies and gentlemen, we must also consider the reality. many families have difficulty getting enough electricity to power their homes. exorbitant increases in energy prices, the effects of the war in ukraine and higher post-corona prices are hitting low-income and lower middle-income earners the hardest. climate transition should be for everyone, not just those who can afford it. after all, not everyone has the financial space to renovate a home, or to invest in an electric car. that is why the social climate fund is so important. because, colleagues, the climate transition will be social or it will not be.

executive vice-president of the commission. – mr president, i would like to use the time allotted to me to talk about an issue that i feel very passionately about. to start with a confession, i am a petrol head. i love cars, i’ve always loved cars, i’ve been repairing and restoring cars since my teenage years. and i still remember every car my dad ever owned and i could even draw it for you. that’s how i was raised. and until today, i still love cars and i love my motorbike. but i also know this love needs to change. i need to go from being a petrol head to becoming a battery head. and i can also say that the fun will not diminish, because the electric cars we see are fun to drive, they’re exciting to drive, they’re also clean, their technology is exciting to follow and europe has finally stepped up to the plate. and europe is now more and more capable to build cars that can compete with the american cars, with the asian cars, even though europe was very late coming to the party. and that is something we can reproach our car industry. bit too much hubris, believing that we would make ever more efficient combustion-engine cars and that would save the day, and now we understand the world has moved on. and i don’t think that by prolonging the change to electric mobility, by leaving a loophole in the legislation so that not a complete ban on emission cars by 2035, but leaving there an opportunity to still build them is not helping the car industry at all. and it certainly is not helping the consumers. if you want our car industry to move faster, to produce affordable electric cars for everyone, you have to make sure they will concentrate everything they do on that. if you want, because then they say: ‘well, it’s not just about the car manufacturers, it’s about this whole ecosystem of suppliers that are part of that’. how are you going to help a supplier who supplies elements for combustion engines? by forcing them to continue to supply those elements in ever decreasing quantities, because that will be the reality. so what you will see, if you choose the so—called 90% option, you will slow down the decrease of prices and the technological development of electric cars. and you will force manufacturers to continue to produce ever-decreasing numbers of combustion engines that will become ever more expensive. that’s an economic logic. it’s a matter of scale. so who are you doing a favour here? no one. and just slowing down technological development is a bad choice. we see it in every industrial revolution. the longer you take to make a change that is inevitable, the more it will cost. it will cost them more money, it will cost more social pain, it will create an illusion. and you chain people to a past that has no future. this is not helping the european car industry at all. and i’m not even speaking about the absolute, absolute certainty that the fuel you think could power these clean combustion engine cars is so much more expensive per kilometre driven than electricity. not just a bit more expensive. no, three, four, five, six times more expensive. if you at least also incorporate in your analysis our plans on rooftop solar, our plans on offshore wind, our plans on creating much, much more opportunity for renewable energy. so don’t deprive european citizens of the possibility to drive a clean, exciting, affordable electric car made in europe. and they say, well, everything has to come from china. nonsense. look at what’s happening in our battery strategy, that’s moving fast forward. we’re becoming fast the first producers of batteries in the future. and these are sustainable batteries, recyclable batteries. look at what we’re doing with solid state batteries, lighter batteries, batteries with more range. look at the plans we have to create a charging infrastructure. this is all very exciting stuff. don’t chain us to the past, for heaven’s sake. don’t chain an industry to the past that needs clarity and not loopholes to do what it needs to do to provide affordable mobility to all europeans. this is, i believe, an essential choice. and i implore you: do the right thing tomorrow. don’t come with a wishy-washy solution that actually helps no one. it gives a bit of comfort – ‘yeah, we gave them a bit more time’ – but it’s not going to help them because then they will have to start building components for the new technology and then still building ever-decreasing numbers of components for the old technology, which will be so expensive. one problem that our battery alliance has is, can we get enough people to work in this industry? that’s our main challenge. so don’t tell me that so many jobs will be lost because of this transition. the jobs will change. our responsibility is to make sure that the people are able to take these new jobs. but there’s no need for anyone to be unemployed thanks to these new and exciting technologies. and since we are the best car-makers in the world, believe this petrol head, since we are the best car-makers in the world, our industry, if we embrace these opportunities, will dictate the future. who knows in this room that poland already today is the biggest producer of electric buses? who knows that? that’s embracing a new technology, that’s embracing the future. that’s the reason why i believe you need to vote for our proposal tomorrow.

in writing. when the fuel is burned in a car's internal combustion engine, the nitrogen in the air and, unfortunately, the sulphur in the fuel, are oxidized at high temperatures. in the air, the oxides form the corresponding acids. the pollution load is particularly high in urban environments. both oxides and acids are harmful to human health. there's a strong correlation between airborne transmission and stillbirth and fetal malformations. therefore, the pollution load from cars should be minimised. administrative interference with the functioning of the market should be kept to a minimum, but at the same time be limited to the extent necessary. i think it is very necessary to encourage the use of electric cars, especially in urban environments with high pollution levels. at the same time, the pollution load dissipates on the roads with little traffic and poses no danger to human health. i have mixed feelings about the ban on the introduction of new cars with internal combustion engines from 2035. the rigid ban will end the development of internal combustion engines much earlier and european manufacturers will no longer be able to compete with other manufacturers. the commission considers that the aid is necessary to ensure that the aid is limited to the minimum necessary to achieve the objective of common interest.
in writing. today we are voting on the commission's proposal to reduce co2 emissions from cars and vans to reach 100% of the zero-emission fleet by 2035, in other words to 'go all-electric' by then. i'm against it for three reasons. this is an environmental mistake because it does not allow other technologies to develop and because the entire battery production cycle would not be taken into account in the co2 calculation. it's a social mistake: i'm not sure that the electric car (ev) will be affordable for everyone in 2035. the gap between the richest and the poorest should not be widened any further. nor is it to increase the gap between the urban and rural population, which is more isolated than ever and lacks infrastructure (charging points). it's a mistake in terms of our strategic autonomy: 90% of lithium is refined in china. i do not want the eu to put its future partly in the hands of the chinese. europe can find its own technological solutions!
the commission has also adopted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. i have just heard you say that supporting the state of self-defence in ukraine from the free world would prolong the war unnecessarily. the question which follows you, mr president, is: what outcome of the war and what scenario after the war do you want? what's your idea?

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the question we are discussing today, namely how our continent can and should attract talent and expertise, is too serious - let me say this - to be the subject of political exploitation. and there are colleagues in this house who recently argued - i could not believe my ears - that obviously talent and expertise can only be found in one part of the world, their own obviously. i'm sorry to disappoint you, but it's not. i am sorry to disappoint you, but the world is full of talent, of competence, of women and men who have been trained, often at great cost, but who can help us to make our europe better. i would like to thank the commission, because this proposal will certainly bear fruit, it will enrich us and the countries of north africa by creating important synergies. but let me say, commissioner, we must also take into account that most of the people who come, who want to reach our countries, do so because they are fleeing from dire situations, from wars, from famine. and we cannot forget that, we cannot turn the other way. so we need to approach the issue of immigration in a holistic way, with the complexity that deserves a serious issue like the one we're talking about today.
mr president, mr vice-president, the european commission's communication and the resolutions of this parliament represent the right approach to managing migration flows by giving priority to the creation of new channels of legal immigration. and whether the far right likes it or not, migration is a natural, positive, necessary and necessary phenomenon. this is a double necessity, justified by the ageing of the european population and by the shortage of workers in many sectors. but their inclusion in our societies is also a cultural, social and political enrichment, as has been shown during the pandemic, and as you, mr vice-president, have recalled. with workers who may come from north africa, but also from other countries, including canada and the united states. last year we took a first step with the approval of the blue card reform; but we need to extend this opportunity to all skill levels and simplify the rest of the legal immigration tools we already have, such as the single permit directive. and lastly, and most importantly, we must work in cooperation with the countries of origin to prevent a brain drain that could impoverish these countries by, please, opting for circular migration.
mr president, mr vice-president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your kind words. i would like to say first of all that i am really extremely happy to see how well and quickly the reception of the refugees from ukraine is going with us, especially thanks to all the volunteers. millions of people have direct access to the labour market from day one and freedom of movement throughout the eu. but let's look at germany for a moment. there are over 1 million workers missing. at the same time, hundreds of thousands of refugees are stuck in asylum procedures, perhaps with tolerated status that doesn't even allow them to work properly, or their qualifications are simply not recognized. and then they end up in odd jobs or stuck in places where there's no work. so, commissioner, please ensure that all those who want to work here can do so, that we put the people who are already here to work, and that the member states and the universities recognise diplomas and other qualifications. and finally, my personal request: please accept my pilot project for a european language learning app, so that the most important skill, which is to understand each other, is already there.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, today we are discussing the european commission's plan for labour migration from egypt, morocco and tunisia. the aim is to make up for the shortage of labour in europe. agreements must also be concluded with senegal, nigeria, pakistan and even bangladesh. already, three million three million non-european foreigners enter the eu every year. but apparently that's not enough for the prophets of the common people. so we are categorically against that plan. and the reason is this. first of all, there are 13 million unemployed in the eu. why should we import migrant workers? secondly, the enormous absorption effect of these immigrant workers is being ignored. because of course they want to bring their families - and if possible their whole families - here, which in many cases will be an additional burden on the already heavy social security system. worse still, the more tunisians, senegalese, pakistanis and so on are allowed to come here, the greater the appeal in the countries of those who remained behind. so the tam-tam will be louder than ever before. so this plan by the christian democrats, socialists and liberals is nonsense. the boat is full.

blue card reply. i am convinced that the eu can and should pursue a migration policy within the existing treaties. trade agreements are a full competence of the european union. the investment protection treaties are a mixed jurisdiction because they confer powers on the member states, namely judicial jurisdiction. however, trade agreements are a full competence which is not exhausted by the union. the french presidency of the council was therefore a very big disappointment in this respect. there is only one trade agreement in existence at the present time, namely with vietnam. we must exhaust our powers, deliver results and create added value for citizens. all this can be done within the framework of the lisbon treaty.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem. president lange has already said that an estimated 25 million people worldwide are victims of forced labour. it is up to us to tackle the importation of products produced by forced labour. such products have no place in our internal market and on the shelves of our shops. we believe that these rules should have been better incorporated in the rules on due diligence. but it's been a while. the united states has therefore adopted an import ban. i have a few reservations about that. firstly, there will be 27 customs authorities and we must therefore be vigilant that rogue importers do not choose the weakest link of those 27. we will also need international cooperation, with a view to the united states. this cooperation must go beyond the fight against illegal imports. in short, we must try to tackle forced labour globally. finally, i would like to draw particular attention to the administrative burden, especially for smes, which will not be able to monitor the entire supply chain, and i would like to call for coherence in the relevant regulation, when it is adopted.

mr president, the council decision this week will be a historic one. it will breathe a new lease on life into the eu’s enlargement strategy. it will present a chance to overcome the long—blocked effort to heal dangerous european divisions and open wounds. this pertains in particular to the countries in the western balkans, most notably albania and north macedonia, but also others. in doing this, in re-invigorating the enlargement policy, we’re not just being gracious. it is in our own interest not to have a grey zone of minor security, a kind of buffer zone between our borders and those of a revisionist russia. not sharing european stability with the eastern partners that want to join the eu family would undermine our own security and our own future. but let me also be explicit about one obvious conditionality for europe’s united perspective: without ukraine’s success in defeating the russian accession, the candidate status promises would fall flat. that’s why providing the necessary arms without hesitation must be enforced.

mr president, this debate on the veto against an agreement on a minimum corporate tax in all the member states, which is the result of a very long mandate of this european parliament, shows firstly that this is a directive and therefore the governments of the member states have the opportunity to assert their tax specificities in the council, where there are, for example, island territories. but to veto on the basis of retaliation against the measures adopted and voted by this european parliament in relation to the rule of law is simply unacceptable. it is therefore clear that the reform of the treaties, as a result of the work of the conference on the future of europe, is inevitable in order to put an end to unanimity and thus establish a decision-making process which will also allow for enlargement. we are talking about the incorporation of new member states. can we support enlargement on the basis of unanimity which makes impossible such a basic principle of tax justice as the large multinationals paying where they make their profits? the answer is simply no.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, once again there is a deadlock in the council. yes, to hide under subsidiarity that taxation is a national state's responsibility is ridiculous, because we are talking about an agreement to end the scourge, to end tax evasion, which is transnational. let's get serious about this. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the tax havens are not used as a means of preventing tax evasion. that is why we in parliament voted massively for this agreement and i very much believe that we cannot get rid of this inequality after all, and i am advocating that pillar 1 and pillar 2 be resolved through this agreement. yes, the role of the commission and the council is to get to implement what we voted for, to defend european citizens, to defend european businesses, small and medium-sized businesses, and to have ethics and fairness, to pay this minimum tax of 15% and the corporate tax where the multinational operates.
member of the commission. – mr president, i think that this debate confirmed how important it is for parliament and for all the european institutions to reach agreement on both pillar one and pillar two. we should never lose sight of what we are talking about. we are talking about establishing a reasonable level of effective minimum taxation at 15%. this would undermine the effectiveness of tax havens and of illegal tax competition, not fair competition, in the european union, with a huge amount of additional revenues for our member states, in a difficult economic situation. second, we are working on pillar one on the target of reallocating taxing rights, because it is no more possible with the digital economy that taxing rights are paid only where companies establish their headquarters and not where they make profits. i think this is very popular, very well understood in our countries. we have the unanimity rule, but the unanimity rule is not something allowing member states to veto something that they have endorsed internationally and they have endorsed at a european level 20 days before. because here we have also another principle in the treaties, which is the principle of loyal cooperation among member states. is this loyal cooperation? i doubt it, honestly. so i appreciate the call for considering the proposal from the conference on the future of europe for qualified majority voting. you know that president von der leyen has referred to this in several speeches. i have heard strong voices calling for exploring the possibility of enhanced cooperation. overall, i think that the eu decision—making process that we respect, because it’s a democratic one, should not be undermined. for this reason, i repeat that, together with you, with the french presidency, with the council, we will not give up!
president of the council. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i could endorse the comments made by paolo gentiloni. i believe that in this shared objective of fair taxation, three elements must be distinguished. firstly, the question that will arise in the medium and long term - rapporteur. – madam president, colleagues, commissioner, if the eu wants to play its role as a global actor, it has to promote rules—based trade and show by example that trade agreements deliver, bring peoples together and raise the standards worldwide on safety of labour, sustainable development goals and human rights. in these geopolitically unstable times, more than ever we have the duty to diversify our supply chains and open up new markets. colleagues, nine years after negotiations were suspended, the political leaders of both the eu and india agreed last year in may to resume negotiations for a balanced, ambitious, comprehensive and mutually—beneficial trade agreement, a separate negotiation on a standalone investment protection agreement and another agreement on geographical indications. this report confirms this approach. an agreement with india will be favourable for economic reasons as the untapped potential is huge. with its 1.3 billion inhabitants, india is the largest democracy in the world and a solid alternative for an eu that wants to diversify its supply chains. although in recent years the eu has become india’s leading trade partner, figures show that the potential in economic cooperation with india remains mainly untapped. india is one out of the eu’s four strategic partners within the european—indo-pacific strategy. therefore, the agreement is also of geopolitical importance. dear colleagues, in this own—initiative report, the european parliament gives its full support to the negotiations, based on the current mandate. meps also firmly welcome the establishment of a trade and technology council with india that should be launched by the end of this year under the czech presidency. we have listed the following positive priorities: a comprehensive free trade agreement with a dedicated chapter on smes on digital trade and raw materials, and an ambitious and enforceable tsd chapter aligned with the paris agreement. we encourage the negotiators to touch upon the longstanding market access irritants both across governance levels and sectors, with the comprehensive elimination of tariffs and quotas on a reciprocal basis, while paying attention to sensitive products and ensuring that reductions will not be compensated by an increase in domestic taxes and levies; a standalone investment protection agreement with a dedicated eu—india investment court system, with a view to the founding of the multilateral investment court; a robust chapter on public procurement applicable at all levels of governance and a prohibition of certain discriminatory bi—national practices; an agreement on the establishment of a bilateral ex-ante and ex-post consultation platform between the eu and india, designed to facilitate discussions or consultations in advance of any new measure that could negatively affect trade or investment; and, finally, we propose – following the good experience we have had in the fta with vietnam – the establishment of a parliamentary joint committee to allow parliamentary oversight. colleagues, i conclude that, without any doubt, challenging negotiations await. however, not only the eu but also india and the world have changed. in all contacts with our indian partners we have clearly understood the political will to succeed. i hope that negotiations can succeed and that this parliament will be able to give its consent in the current mandate. we therefore need to grab this opportunity to strengthen eu—india ties with both hands. finally, i would like to thank my entire colleagues for the good cooperation, and i hope for the approval of my report with a broad majority tomorrow.
president of the commission you talk about the state of emergency and the importance of care in europe. and that's true, of course. but let us not be blind, let us not be silent here, that this is the result of concrete policy. first and foremost, the austerity policy which you have imposed on the member states for years. and today, market forces are being introduced more and more into health care. market forces are at odds with good public health care, which is about saving costs and maximising profits. then the staff must do it with fewer and fewer resources and fewer and fewer staff. and then the people who need to be cared for are only allowed to shower once a week and they don't get enough hygiene supplies and so on. so we have to break with the market forces. the solution is more public investment and a clear break with the policy you have pursued so far. i expect that from any european health care strategy: break with the logic of privatization and guarantee mass good public care.
in writing. over the past two years, our carers have shown great commitment, professionalism and perseverance in providing the best possible care for everyone. clearly, quality care, for both the providers and the patients, must be a top priority. the sector deserves more than applause. the number of persons in the eu requiring long-term care is expected to increase from 30,8 million in 2019 to 38,1 million in 2050. the european parliament therefore approved this ambitious report which calls for a long-term strategy for our care sector. i fully support this report, with particular attention to making care jobs more attractive: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. this report is also groundbreaking in that it recognises the need for care. 80% of all long-term care in europe is provided by unpaid carers. these are 40 to 50 million europeans who care for family or friends. they often do so without official status, without a fixed number of leave days, a care allowance or clear pension rules. i am therefore very pleased that this report provides for a statute based on ambitious european standards.
in writing. madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, there is an urgent need to support the whole healthcare sector. health is priceless! but the health crisis has shown the weakness of our health care systems, in part due to chronic underinvestment. the problem is twofold. there is a lack of quality, accessible and affordable long-term care services and a lack of recognition of workers in the sector, who are prone to absenteeism and worker absenteeism. however, demographic change, population ageing, as well as climate change and mitigation reforms will exacerbate the pressure on the sector. i therefore support the report on a common european action on care and call on the commission to take swift action, especially as it would be in line with the conclusions of the conference on the future of europe. finally, there is a need to ensure rapid and equal access to high quality care services for people of all generations and to support carers through, inter alia, wage increases, contract adjustments and recognition of the physical and psychological burdens of the environment.
member of the commission. – mr president, i first want to thank the honourable members for this enriching debate. i think we are all sharing the fact that especially households, but also companies, smes, are suffering from these price increases. and we are all convinced that we have to act boldly to mitigate this situation. when we are talking about the causes, i’m a bit surprised. because first it seems that we have forgotten that before the war there was a pandemic. and this pandemic has already pushed prices high because a lot of value chains have been interrupted. when you talk to some industries, they cannot get the material they need. so there is a price hike which is due to the pandemic. the second one is that we are moving into something which is quite close to a war economy. and we should call it like that, that the situation is not a normal situation which can be easily resolved. and we know where the responsibility lies. the responsibility first lies there where the war has started, where the decision to aggress an independent country has started and was taken. and i think that we just cannot give in to such an aggression, which would then be an invitation to pursue this kind of aggression. now we all know that in an exceptional period markets do not normally function. and we are driven in many areas not by normally functioning markets, but by incredible speculative movements, which has pushed food and energy up. and when you look at the inflation, it’s mainly pushed by the price hikes of energy and food beside the causes i’ve mentioned on the value chains. and those who now say that it’s because of the green deal that we have inflation. they just do not tell the truth because we have not yet seen finally some real impact of the green deal. we see now that even coal factories are relaunched. so we have not yet seen any real impact on this by the green deal. but what we know, by the way, is that due to the war, climate change is continuing. in writing. the european project was born of solidarity and the need to move forward by implementing sound social and economic policies. to date, the eu has been a beacon in the world as a more advanced rights-based bloc, with decades of social progress bearing witness to its success and expansion. however, if the eu does not offer a better life for the majority, we run the risk of people turning their backs on the project. we call on the member states and the commission to take ambitious measures that demonstrate their determination to ensure a decent standard of living, sustainable and inclusive growth and upward social convergence. in times of crisis, only a renewed focus on europe's integrated social, environmental and economic project will ensure its future success. we are convinced that putting the well-being of people at the centre of all policies is both a realistic goal and an ethical imperative of our time. we socialists are committed to a different recovery, one that prioritises progress and social justice, social and civil dialogue and respect for fundamental social rights as the basis for the ecological and digital transitions.

in writing. europe is experiencing an economic crisis which will have a negative impact on everyone, but especially on ordinary people. we, therefore, as parliament, must do everything possible to mitigate the impact of the crisis on our citizens. the european economy has not yet recovered from the pandemic crisis, but it is once again being hit hard by rising inflation, rising energy and fuel prices, and higher prices on store shelves, which are driving up the cost of living. rising inflation is having a particularly negative impact on household budgets - and this is being felt strongly by europeans. according to eu agencies, food prices in the eu will rise by about 20% in the autumn. the high price increases will affect all member states, but the impact will be much more pronounced in the so-called new countries, including lithuania. food accounts for about 30 percent of all household expenses, and that number is growing. to protect the people most at risk of poverty, the union should therefore create a special 'anti-inflation shield' to reduce the rise in prices, which in the euro area is much faster than the incomes of citizens. this reduces living standards and forces households to cut back on spending. people in need need of assistance should be given access to social housing or housing assistance. disadvantaged people have the right to adequate financial assistance and support, and ordinary people must be protected from the effects of the crisis.
in writing. as a hungarian social democrat, i have long held the view that the european community should finally turn left after the right-liberal-conservative trend that has been dominant for years and put the human being at the centre. the covid pandemic and the accompanying economic crisis, which has been exacerbated by the war situation, are also hitting member states with a deepening social crisis, depending on their resilience. the crisis has shown that without solidarity and cooperation, there are no meaningful and lasting solutions. and social crisis can only be addressed with social solutions. there are already many examples in member states from spain to romania of how social measures funded by eu funds can help families who have been made homeless by the crisis. unfortunately, the hungarian government has not yet recognized, or has become so insensitive in its anti-european struggle, that there are eu instruments that can provide a temporary lifeline for citizens: for example, by financing the food aid with the opportunity coupon proposed by the mszp. the hungarian government should review and revise its national plan submitted in connection with the recovery fund as soon as possible in order to reinforce the social-based crisis management measures. the european union has an important role to play in pressing member state governments to implement the social pillars, which have been adopted but not fully adopted, as widely as possible. it is time to put people at the centre of europe and not just economic interests.
the next item is the report by mr jordi cañas on the eu action plan for the social economy (2021/2179 (ini)) (a9-0192/2022) i would remind you that the seats are vacant, except for the first two rows, which are allocated to the heads of the political groups. you can request 'catch the eye' and 'blue card' cards via your voting device after you have inserted your voting card. i would also like to remind you that speeches in the chamber will continue to be made from the central platform, except for spontaneous requests for the floor, blue cards and points of order.
member of the commission. – mr president, first, i am very pleased to see parliament’s support and engagement towards the development of the social economy. we all recognise the important role of the social economy in our economies and societies, as it puts fairness and sustainability as its core values. working for an economy that works indeed for people. today, our societies face major social, environmental, democratic and economic challenges. the covid-19 crisis, the green and digital transitions and the war in ukraine are largely affecting our resilience and social cohesion, as the previous debate has illustrated. in this particular context, the social economy has a key role to play to make our society fairer and more inclusive. the social economy represents a strong social and economic reality in several member states providing quality jobs, contributing to the overall economic activity, particularly at local and regional level, and to the provision of care and social services. in fact, more than a third of the estimated 13.8 million paid jobs in the social economy is in the sector of social services. social economy organisations also contribute to social and labour market inclusion of disadvantaged groups like people with disabilities or migrants. they help to improve gender equality and integrate young people. they also drive sustainable economic and industrial development, promoting the active participation of citizens in our societies, and revitalise europe’s rural and depopulated areas. however, the social economy potential is not fully tapped as its business model is not evenly spread across the european union. while in a number of eu member states, it is still largely underdeveloped and the action plan put forward by the commission aims to allow all relevant public authorities and stakeholders to act jointly and fully use the potential of the social economy using also and benefiting from the european dimension, be it financial but also the european internal market. the action plan presents more than 60 measures to unlock this potential with three key objectives. first, the development of an enabling framework for the social economy. policy and legal frameworks need to be adapted to this specific business model, including in specific areas such as state aid, public procurement and taxation. and in this regard, the proposal for a council recommendation will be key. it will set out guidelines on the policies and legal frameworks. member states need to put in place to support the development of the social economy, providing targeted guidance while taking into account the different traditions, scope and denomination of the social economy in the different member states. secondly, the opening of opportunities through enhanced support to capacity building and skills, as well as improved access to funding. thirdly, enhancing the recognition of the social economy and its potential by promoting a better understanding of its specificities to all relevant stakeholders. i want to thank the rapporteur and the shadows for your important report. parliament is fully supporting the ambition of the action plan and the commission is very grateful for that. in particular, you share the commission’s view that policymakers and stakeholders need to work on improving all aspects of the social economy ecosystem jointly. your report also includes several concrete suggestions on the way we should implement the action plan. these are very valuable to us as we are busy preparing several key initiatives. please be assured that we will duly take those suggestions into account. your ideas on the scope of the future council recommendation on developing social economy framework conditions; your call for the role of the social economy to be fully recognised in the context of other policies and future initiatives, such as the care strategy we discussed this morning; your suggestions on our future study to collect qualitative and quantitative information on the social economy across all member states; your consideration for the transition pathway for the proximity and social economy industrial ecosystem which is under preparation; and your call for new initiatives to support the development of worker buy outs. we also recall the european parliament’s recent legislative own initiative resolution on the statute for european cross-border associations and non-profit organisations. the commission committed in its reply to the parliament of may 22 to come up with a package of legislative and non-legislative measures to address the parliament’s objectives. we will continue engaging with you on this important matter. dear president, your ideas and support are very valuable to us in our work on the implementation of the action plan. but they are also valuable to the millions of people working every day in social enterprises, social entities and making our society function a bit better. social economy can play a decisive role to support the shift to a sustainable and resilient growth model, as proposed by the conference on the future of europe.
on behalf of the id group. mr president, commissioner, austerity, social unrest, cuts in public services: a few years ago the eu did not go to the trouble of trying to stop the leaking euro-titanic. while the little music of the return to budgetary austerity is being played, the european union is drowning the fish with texts like this one. when you call for - and i quote - the commission to promote the social economy internationally, you have to be really careful not to laugh. the reality is that the bulky ship 'commission' is sailing blindly, encountering galley after galley, flooding our markets with cheap manufactured goods imported by container ships across seas and oceans. how far away is the social economy! we no longer have time to waste words by adding commas to reports which ultimately have only one objective: to keep the commission in line while the purchasing power of the french is declining. in the face of the tide, against the winds and the tides, patriots are the only ones who keep the course of the nation that protects.
on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, commissioner, the social economy is a new model compared with the traditional one, which can cover a reality intermediate between the public and the private sector, generating new opportunities that respond to a social good. the typology and development of entities recognised within this model in the european area has been very heterogeneous. therefore, the uniqueness and regulation of each nation must be respected. we do not agree that the wealth that the social economy can bring, the creativity that has yet to be developed or the potential are affected by an imposition, by a requirement to align or condition these projects to the objectives of other agendas, such as the environment, or the sustainable development goals, which limit entrepreneurship in only one direction. the social economy must help generate activity and services - needs that are not being met - but at the same time it must coexist with a market economy, never devour it. it must do so while respecting free competition and offering the same quality standards, without requiring exclusive privileges that would deprive other business projects in the same sector of presence or competitiveness.
the social economy action plan is particularly important for the 14 million workers involved in this sector. in recent years we have seen a rapid growth of the sector, with an increasing importance of social enterprise in our society. the action plan was needed to ensure that the sector could continue to grow in a sustainable and social way in the future. i would like to stress three important elements: first, a review of the existing state aid rules. the current rules too often put a brake on the functioning of social enterprises. this is why this relaxation of regulations is so necessary in order to allow tailor-made companies to grow. secondly, we must continue to focus on socially responsible public procurement. these public procurement contracts represent 14% of european gdp and can therefore be an important tool for achieving the social objective of the european social pillar. thirdly, a clear european definition will make it easier for genuine social enterprises to differentiate themselves, both in public procurement and in access to european funds. this action plan should give a social boost to our labour market, so that people who are often excluded from the regular labour market can participate in our society in a meaningful and full way.

mr president, commissioner, mr president of the council, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! deforestation and forest degradation must be combated by all means possible. our forests around the world are our green lungs. we have to protect her. they make a valuable contribution to our climate by reducing co2 emissions, if they are also managed sustainably. the regulation provides for the most extensive due diligence and documentation obligations. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the market is not affected by the new rules. i would just like to make a critical remark: i believe that we should not exaggerate with these various conditions and obligations, especially for those who import and who participate in trade here. we are not trying to build a massive compliance system, but we are trying to get to the root of the problem. i therefore believe that we must continue to think about alternatives and other measures. so this is a proposal which will hopefully be evaluated with some measure of restraint when we vote tomorrow. but i think it's not the only way, and we should also continue to work on alternatives.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, let me first of all thank you for packaging the debates on the forest strategy and on global deforestation, because at the end of the day, these are two sides of the same coin. and i have listened carefully to the different interventions tonight, and i take good note of your ambition, and your comments and of the impressions you took back from your recent travels to amazonia. let me briefly address just a couple of points raised by several of you. first of all, several of you have mentioned the rights of indigenous people. and i can only share your concerns that deforestation and forest degradation have a dramatic impact on the livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, including indigenous peoples who rely heavily on forest ecosystems and are the best defenders of forests. the regulation will ensure that no commodity or products issued from deforestation or forest degradation of the lands inhabited by indigenous people is allowed to enter the eu market. the cooperation structures foreseen in the regulation should allow the full participation of all stakeholders, including civil society, indigenous people and local communities; partnerships will strengthen the rights of forest—dependent communities. we will, of course, closely look into your and the member states’ position on these issues and assess the co—legislators’ position. however, let me stress that whenever considering additional references, we must also take into account that other legislative initiatives – in particular the corporate sustainability due diligence directive – also comprehensively address this issue, so as to avoid any duplication. i heard also your strong calls to extend the scope of the regulation to other commodities. let me briefly reiterate the commission position on this matter and our concerns. the six commodities included in our draft regulation have been proposed taking into account the impact assessment of the initiative. they already cover over 80% of products that contribute to deforestation, and if we propose new products, we need proof that they are really the ones where the union is contributing the most to the global deforestation. and as i mentioned in my introduction, without that proof, our regulation could be challenged, especially at the international level. and we of course should avoid that by all means. our proposal therefore sets out a progressive scope that will be updated regularly, according to new deforestation trends and data. and again, the aim of progressive scope is to focus first on the commodities where the eu’s impact on global deforestation and forest degradation is the largest, and the regulatory intervention promises to have the most impact. some final comments also on financial institutions, which are proposed to be included in the scope of the regulation. the european union has several laws already in place or under development that address the environmental responsibility of financial institutions. however, the regulatory system functions via entirely different authorities. therefore i need to make you aware that including them in our proposal would require setting up two completely different tracks for monitoring and enforcement. in the area of sustainable finance in the sustainable finance disclosure regulation, financial market participants and financial advisers are required to disclose adverse impacts on sustainability matters at entity and financial products levels. the revised corporate sustainability reporting directive, as well as the broader sustainable finance framework that is being put in place, will significantly improve transparency, and complement and support the legislative initiative on deforestation. so our proposal is forward looking and it is designed to prevent further deforestation. it is an opportunity to level the playing field for the many businesses who are already cleaning up their supply chains. it is a chance to underline the eu’s leadership in addressing urgent global problems – not just deforestation, but forest degradation as well. but we also need to move fast. that matters because we are confident that when others see our system working, they too will follow. in the coming weeks and months, the commission will work with all of you to deliver an outcome that is ambitious, realistic and easy to put in place. that means protecting the essential elements of this pioneering proposal: coverage of deforestation in all of its forms, both legal and illegal, strict traceability, strong due diligence, good definitions based on international work. and lastly, it means avoiding loopholes wherever possible, and appropriate coverage for the largest traders. the commission has been supporting the technical discussion from the outset. we stand ready to continue working with you and the council as an honest broker to find compromises that are agreeable to all. with matching levels of commitment on all sides, i very much hope to see a solid agreement before the end of this year.

in writing. ladies and gentlemen, i shall vote in favour of the proposal for a regulation on the making available on the union market and the export from the union of certain basic products and products associated with deforestation and forest degradation. the eu needs to reduce its share of global deforestation, which mainly occurs through its supply chains. the european market must become a market with zero deforestation. to this end, it is necessary to extend the scope of the regulation to any product contributing to deforestation, including maize and products derived from palm oil. it is important to remember that controls will be essential and will be done through vigilance duties for any producer or operator intending to import/export to the eu market. finally, i would urge a vote in favour of including financial institutions in the regulation, given the link between their investments and global deforestation. this report is of great importance, and it is important to support it so that the world's forests remain a source of biodiversity, livelihoods and life for all.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, let us not go around the bush. let us assume that the energy market is a failure and that opening up this sector to competition is one of the worst decisions taken and imposed by the european union on the member states. this market allows, in times of crisis, for some energy companies to produce electricity at €40 per megawatt to sell it quietly at €1,000 per megawatt, for maximum profit for shareholders, at the expense of millions of people who will no longer be able to pay heating bills and who are shamefully asked to wear a collar if they are cold in winter. in the face of such a scandal, the measures proposed by the commission and the council are not up to the task. what we need is to fully recover our ability to make our own decisions in the energy field. we must move away from the european energy market to a public energy hub whose action will be dictated solely by the general interest. public management that protects the most vulnerable, that plans according to needs, that uses the revenues from production to deploy renewable energy, that defends our energy sovereignty against foreign powers. energy is a common good that should no longer be monopolized and that we must manage and protect.
madam president, on monday an implementing act of the european commission comes into force, which is a death blow to the spanish fishing industry, one more blow. i sincerely believe that the commission has exceeded its powers and we also have doubts whether the relevant and most up-to-date scientific reports have been taken into account. the commission says yes, that it does so on the basis of the data which it has received and that it has not received any more up-to-date data. the member states, and spain in particular, say that they did, that they contributed. in any case, the situation is very serious. the commission has to explain with data and dates whether the duties have been done and whether the rules have been complied with. also, if there's been any kind of negligence that's caused us to be in this situation. in any case, whoever is responsible, those who are certainly not responsible are our fishermen, and that is why we must find a solution now. the application of this act can and must be suspended and must be suspended before monday.
mr president, last week i was in antwerp and met workers who were building a new factory at borealis. they were recruited by an italian company, irem. these are proud people, skilled people with experience and people who help build our country and our continent. they came from bangladesh, turkey, ukraine. before they came here, they were lured with all kinds of false promises and sometimes even false contracts through a chain of subcontractors; they went through hungary, without work, without pay, to portugal. there, too, no work, no pay, hardly any money to eat. then in antwerp: exploited and threatened. eleven hours a day, six days a week! yes, it is absolutely inconceivable that this could happen within the european union, and certain rules within the european union make it easier! for example, why do we not limit the number of levels of subcontracting to one level? or why don't we make the prime contractor responsible for wages and working conditions? i mean, it's got to be now, right?
president, commissioner, people with disabilities belong in our society. not on the edge, not on the sidelines, but in the middle! they want to be able to participate in economic, social and cultural life like everyone else. they do not want to be pampered from a caring perspective, but to be able to develop their talents and skills to the full. unfortunately, they still face too many obstacles. for example, people with reduced mobility in several member states have problems taking public transport. people with hearing or vision problems often have difficulty finding or keeping a job. people with disabilities sometimes have to move across the country to find suitable accommodation. so the rudder must be turned: this has to change! that is why we support the proposal, commissioner, to set up a european centre, 'accessibleeu'. here's an example of how it should be done: the pilot project will also provide information on the impact of the accessibility of the internet on the health of citizens and the environment, as well as on the impact of the use of the internet on the health of citizens and the environment.

vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. your honour, you refer to the agreement with azerbaijan to increase our gas supply. yes, it does. azerbaijan is giving us, no, it's selling us, naturally, nobody gives anything for free in the gas market today, it's giving us, it gave us last year, even before these incidents, 8 million cubic feet of natural gas. and this year, thanks to this agreement, we are going to get a substantial increase from 8 to 12, that is, a 50% increase. this has not been in return for any political concession in the conflict and i am sure the europeans will appreciate that in the present circumstances the president of the commission, herself, has travelled to azerbaijan to negotiate this agreement, which offers the possibility that this winter all europeans as a whole will have the necessary energy supply. that gas will go through the southern corridor. it will not be received by all europeans, but i am sure that those who will receive it will be very pleased. in any case, i can assure you that this energy agreement has not been to the detriment of any human rights or democratic problems that have arisen in that country.

on behalf of the vpc/hr. – mr president, honourable members of the european parliament, as you have been able to follow over the past few days, the situation has been particularly tense in burkina faso. we have witnessed what we can call a coup de force by captain traoré, who brought together a large part of the army to overthrow the president of the transition, damiba. for the record, the latter had already come to power on 24 january 2022, thanks to a military coup overthrowing president kaboré. for the european union, this coup in the coup is particularly regrettable. we had strongly supported ecowas in their mediation efforts to find a way forward with the new burkinabe authorities. we had engaged politically with the transition authorities to encourage them on this path, with commitments to reengage. the hr/vp had met president damiba in new york two weeks ago and we were ready to boost our cooperation with the country, including in the field of defence and security, while continuing our support to the people of burkina faso. important discussions are taking place between ecowas and the new authorities in burkina faso as we speak. we look forward to these discussions leading to positive and constructive conclusions, which respect the commitments made at the time of the removal of president damiba. if these agreements are respected and there is a clear sense of good will, we will need to grasp it and show in a firm and convincing way that we stand together with the people of burkina faso in fighting terrorism, combating the humanitarian crisis and supporting the return to constitutional order. certainly, burkina faso will need external support. the current challenge is to convince the new junta that, even if the wagner option is tempting, those mercenaries are objectively ineffective in the fight against terrorism in the sahel, and we can witness this more and more in mali every day. so, colleagues, we will need some time to assess whether burkina faso and the european union can continue as partners. we will work to do our part to sustain our strong support to the people of burkina faso.
on behalf of the ppe group. - mr president, madam commissioner, i have to ask you to leave. the last time we had a debate here in february on the situation in burkina faso, commissioner dali spoke on behalf of the commission and said that the union would respond to the changed situation in burkina faso, encourage a return to democracy and make the fight against terrorism a priority. it must be mrs. let's face it, this is real. the union's action so far has produced very little result. the intent was there. in june, religious extremists carried out an attack in pansy that killed 100 people, causing thousands to flee the area. and last week, the successful military takeover of ouagadougou by captain ibrahim traoré overthrew the previous coup. this is a very worrying situation. but it must be seen that this is not a problem specific to burkina faso. in west africa, this was the seventh successful military takeover since 2020. the successive coups d'état have destabilised the entire sub-saharan region, giving rise to the growth of religious extremism, including the local islamic state group. we recognise that the real potential for eu action lies in the coordination of rtd as a multiplier between member states and international organisations, and between local partners. i repeat, the coordination. the european union cannot do anything about the situation on its own. madam high representative, madam commissioner, i have to ask you to leave. what opportunities do you see for this coordinated action? how do you know that the union intends to coordinate credibly the action of the various international and eu actors in the current situation in burkina faso?
for the greens/efa group mr president, madam commissioner, the sahel is a major concern for europe. what is happening in burkina faso today is a sign of the political collapse of the states in the region. the french military intervention in mali in 2013, when the islamic state was already in power in syria and iraq, prevented a second terrorist hotbed from being established in the sahel. this intervention was necessary. the big mistake that followed was made by the european union, which left france isolated. if europe had intervened more, it would have cut short the hostile propaganda linked to france's colonial past. the malian and burkinabe states have been seriously destabilized in recent years by the rise of islamist armed groups. each coup is a new signal of their failure in the fight against terrorism, and the frantic pace of these repeated coups indicates an end to the cycle. all of this could unfortunately lead to a decisive victory for the islamists. so what do you do? europe will have to be operational as soon as the coup-plotters have finally failed. those who are ready to take over must be assured of a cooperation framework which is immediately operational. if this vast area of the sahel falls permanently into the hands of islamists, europe will inevitably suffer the consequences. we therefore call on the external action service to coordinate the initiative and on the member states to provide it with means of action, including military, which are not limited to those of the french army alone, which is vulnerable to smear campaigns given its colonial past.

for the greens/efa group mr president, commissioner, the revision of the sustainable development chapters in trade treaties is a significant step, it must be acknowledged, towards more sustainable international trade, involving civil society and parliament more closely and setting out mechanisms for implementation and monitoring and sanctions for non-compliance with the provisions contained therein. but there are still "buts". with all due respect, commissioner, i hope that this parliament has no reason to doubt the seriousness with which the commission and dg trade are looking at this matter. i was expecting an exchange with the trade commissioner because, while the 15-point action plan is, i repeat, a step forward, there are still problems and your answers to the questions put by our president, mr lange, are far from satisfactory in my view, particularly in the context of the implementation roadmaps. i would like to understand how the commission intends to follow the logic to the end if, for example, one of its trading partners fails to honour its commitments. what sanctions will be applied? what will be the mechanism? will we go through a dispute settlement mechanism? another dimension that was highlighted during the various exchanges was the issue of human rights, which is not part of the chapters on sustainable development. we believe that this is a fundamental shortcoming, since it is well known that, unfortunately, many human rights violations still occur in the commercial environment. the ombudsman has recently raised this issue, and she proposes to set up a full-fledged, parallel mechanism, which would also be open to stakeholders from our trading partners. here again, i have a very specific request to the commission: how does the commission intend to follow up this proposal from the ombudsman? i think that would be a real and substantial complement to this revision of the sustainable development chapters in our trade agreements.
