the agenda includes the debate on the commission statement on the international day for the eradication of poverty (2022/2878 ((rsp)).
member of the commission. – madam president, ‘end poverty in all its forms everywhere’ is the first of the 17 un sustainable development goals. according to the united nations global compact, more than 700 million people – or 10% of the global population – still live in extreme poverty. i am glad that parliament decided to remind everyone that today is the international day for the eradication of poverty, and i very much welcome this debate. poverty is the root cause of many human rights and labour rights violations. for example, child labour, forced labour and human trafficking are each deeply connected to poverty. when it comes to the european union, according to eurostat figures, in 2021, there were an estimated 95.4 million people in the eu at risk of poverty or social exclusion, which was equivalent to 21% of the total population: a really frightening figure. according to arope rates, up until last year, relative poverty – linked to insufficient income – was declining from 2016 to 2018, but picked up again in 2019 and 2020. when it comes to adequate income, having a job is increasingly not necessarily a viable route out of poverty. in 2019, almost 1 in 10 workers experienced in-work poverty, a situation that is absolutely inacceptable. the covid-19 pandemic halted the positive trend in poverty reduction over the past 25 years. the combined impacts of russia’s war of aggression towards ukraine and of climate change may be further detrimental if we do not take bold measures to support the most vulnerable, but also those households that are suffering from skyrocketing energy price increases without seeing their income being adapted to this evolution. the european pillar of social rights and the porto targets put combating poverty at their core. the union strategy to address poverty is reflected in the european pillar of social rights and its principles, whose objectives are providing equal opportunities for all, fair working conditions and social protection to fight against poverty and inequalities. lifting at least 15 million people, including at least 5 million children, out of poverty and social exclusion is our joint european target, proposed by the commission in its action plan for the implementation of the pillar, and welcomed last year at the porto social summit and endorsed by the european council. we have to stick to this objective because poverty is hitting more and more families. all eu member states have set their own national targets to reduce poverty, adding up and exceeding the eu targets. prior to russia’s invasion of ukraine, social indicators for 2021 mainly flagged up positive changes. this was thanks to the contribution of social protection and social inclusion systems. the at-risk-of-poverty or social exclusion rate for children remains higher than the rate for the general population. this is a real challenge, as children growing up in poverty or social exclusion are less likely to do well in school, enjoy good health and realise their full potential later in life. this is something we just cannot accept. young people have been strongly impacted by the covid-19 pandemic. in addition, young people are generally less well covered by social protection systems, resulting in a higher risk of poverty or social exclusion for 16 to 29 year olds. they often face job precariousness and a lack of affordable housing. while latest data confirm an improvement in the poverty and relative income situation of older people, older women face a significantly higher poverty risk and lower pensions than men do. women’s lower earnings and under—representation in the labour market translate into their higher risk of poverty. other factors make women more vulnerable, such as single parenthood, disability and a migrant background. women are also very often over—represented in sectors where wages are low and precariousness is high, and they are very often obliged to work part—time. russia’s war of aggression against ukraine has caused a clear deterioration of the socio-economic outlook, and it has triggered a humanitarian crisis, with millions of ukrainians having fled their country. at the same time, the criminal destruction of mainly civil infrastructure in ukraine has pushed poverty up dramatically in this country. against this critical context and key challenges, strong action is needed. we need to continue to tackle the source of the soaring energy prices, which are driving high inflation. we need to prevent households falling into a precarious economic situation, in particular lower—income families. we also need to review the adequacy and coverage of social safety nets, in particular, minimum income benefits and access to enabling services. wage policies and adequate minimum wages are key to countering poverty, in particular in-work poverty, and to protecting households’ purchasing power. i call on all member states to swiftly transpose and implement – even before transposition – the directive on minimum wages. i must say i have noticed that a certain number of member states have now increased their minimum wages, by more than 5, 7 or 10%. other countries have indexed their minimum wages. to effectively fight against poverty and inequality, we need an integrated approach based on the coordination of different measures and policies. the commission has recently adopted a communication that provides guidance to member states on making greater use of distributional impact assessments, and to better target the different measures. concrete initiatives are put forward through the implementation of the european pillar of social rights. the objective of the european child guarantee is to break the vicious circle of intergenerational poverty. now it’s time to put this child guarantee into practice, to implement it through national action plans. the recently adopted european care strategy – a sector where an overall majority of women are working – gives not only children a better education and better care but also supports long-term care systems. finally, the council recommendation on adequate minimum income ensuring active inclusion education and skills are key in finding good jobs with a decent wage, and so help to reduce poverty. addressing energy poverty is a key part of the approach to the fight against poverty. with parliament, we are working on combating homelessness, a form of extreme poverty which affects 700 000 people or more in europe. the commission recently put forward a regulation on an emergency intervention to address high energy prices which sets a revenue cap on companies that produce electricity at a lower cost. therefore, we also propose that excessive profits should go to support households and companies. we need a holistic approach to tackle poverty and inequalities. we have to make sure social policies are considered on an equal footing as other policies, be it economic, fiscal or taxation policies. fairness is one of the four strands of this commission economic strategy. these are constituent elements of our unique social market economy. but we europeans cannot ignore that hundreds of millions of people live in extreme poverty outside europe. they are struggling to survive. this year, about 5 million children have already died from poverty. today, on the international day for the eradication of poverty, more than 11 000 kids will have died from hunger, absence of health care and disastrous living conditions. this is not fate. this could be avoided. our commitment has to be clear and solid. social justice is the condition and foundation of peace. therefore, the eu should be at the forefront of building a fair, just and sustainable world economy that eradicates poverty.
the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to take action against poverty. but people in poverty deserve our attention not just today, but every day of the year. poverty still affects 91 million people including 18 million children in europe. the cost of living in the form of energy, housing and food is skyrocketing. people are barely surviving, and the purchasing power crisis is affecting not only the lower income but also the lower middle income. this is completely unacceptable. we have committed ourselves to lifting 15 million people out of poverty by 2030. we must and will realize this. this is why the roll-out of the social pillar action plan is so essential. it is also good that important dossiers such as the directive on the european minimum wage have been successfully concluded. we must also give our full support to future dossiers on a social climate fund, platform workers and pay transparency. and just like that, we must continue the fight against homelessness and for affordable housing. so it cannot be limited to words and good intentions. we need an integrated and structural anti-poverty strategy that tackles the causes of poverty and makes the eu more resilient to future crises. we owe that to our europeans. poverty has no place in our society. as you yourself once said in a webinar: poverty belongs in a museum. europe will be social or it will not be.
mr president, of course, we are here today for justice for daphne caruana galizia, a journalist who was just doing her work and who was murdered in malta, with a bomb in a car. she was killed with complicity of the state and of some rich people. today, only those who executed the orders are in jail. what about those who requested and who called for the murder? why is her family still a target of slapps? of course, we are here today for justice for daphne caruana galizia. but we are also here for what she was fighting for: we are here for press freedom, for democratic values, for the right of the people to know what is going on. this is what daphne caruana galizia gave her life for – for the fight for democracy to survive. democracy and the rule of law cannot survive without a free press. that’s why we must protect it.
mr president, commissioner, today we must once again applaud the work of ngos, but also of investigative journalists. as their activities are increasingly criminalised, without them the olaf report, which describes the actions of the frontex agency, would never have been published. and without them and without the press, today's debate would never have been possible or public. frontex and its former management have benefited from the complacency and laissez-faire of the member states, too happy to see the borders of fortress europe rise, borders that are increasingly murderous. i am also very happy that i don't have to show solidarity and humanity in welcoming the exiles. the olaf report is overwhelming. in addition to its many internal dysfunctions, frontex has knowingly - knowingly - ignored violations of fundamental rights. frontex has knowingly let national authorities actively engage in illegal returns, in total contradiction to international and european law. the most funded agency of the european union has lied to this parliament and is increasingly openly flouting european values. we refuse to allow european taxpayers' money to be used to finance these actions. that is why tomorrow this parliament must refuse to grant frontex discharge.
president, commissioner, is there no loading dock at the loading dock? will my charging card be accepted? load stress: a phenomenon familiar to many electric riders. and for me too. and so my hope is that this plan will make this stress a thing of the past. and it is not just road users who need to be relieved of their worries. our shipping lanes and airports also deserve the right infrastructure. i also really realize that this is going to be a challenge. think of the limited electrical grid and the trouble with paying. this is why member states should start now, as far as the sdc is concerned, to strengthen the electricity grid and work on smart technologies, such as smart charging. because that's how we can relieve the pressure on the power grid. payment should also be made easier, with a payment card or a charging card, so that charging is accessible anywhere in the eu . i see that the transport sector is working very hard to achieve that sustainable future, but it cannot happen overnight. so give them a realistic timeframe. we must now do what is necessary to make the energy supply for the transport sector future-proof. and to get that right, i'm calling for a national coordinator to oversee the rollout and help local governments with that challenge. because one thing is clear to me: these proposals do not mean that we should drive less, sail less or fly less, but that we should be cleaner.
mr president, madam commissioner, rapporteur and colleagues, in order to reach our climate targets, we must work to decarbonise the transport sector, as we know it is the only sector where emissions have increased. at the same time, it is important to remember that the maritime sector, like aviation, they are global industries, so unilateral changes that could hinder the competitiveness of european companies must be avoided. there are also challenges, as we know, regarding the availability of renewable maritime fuels. as such, i am glad that the parliament’s position here is ambitious but also realistic. it is vital that we progressively increase the share of low-carbon fuels, respect technology neutrality and ensure necessary incentives for funding research and development in this area. while we are creating these europe-wide regulations, we must take the special conditions of member states also into account. for finland, this included the compensation formula for ice navigation. i commend the rapporteur and my colleagues for taking this into consideration and including it in the final report.
mr president, commissioner, i would like to congratulate all the rapporteurs on our cooperation and the positive result of this work. the negotiations have not been easy, but we are now voting for new safety nets in the shipping sector, taking into account regional specific needs, such as possible exemptions for island regions for which the only viable and affordable solution - at least for the time being - comes from shipping. it is also extremely important that the need for coordination with the international maritime organisation, the imo, is recognised, and although we can and will be more ambitious. it is also important that the 'polluter pays' principle has been strengthened. and i will continue in english because i think it is very important to reject plenary amendments that wish to water down the requirements of the rfnbo sub-targets. in fact, while the 2% minimum use target set for 2030 is a good start, but we could aim even higher for the following years as we are going to have reviews of this regulation and then we can also increase that. there are good examples, like renewable hydrogen from nonbiological sources like solar panels, that is expected to play a key strategic role in the decarbonisation of the generally hard-to-decarbonise transport sector.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, as you know, 90% of goods in europe pass through the sea. shipping accounts for 13% of co2 emissions from transport and is an unprecedented nuisance when it comes to air quality. and more and more cities, like marseille, ajaccio and all over europe, are mobilizing against these super-paquebots that pollute the air. we have solutions, and i'm asking you to vote for them. not to make gas and lng another energy transition. we will not achieve our carbon neutrality goals with fossil fuels. it's easy: let's get off gas, especially the energy mix. we must focus on hydrogen, as the european commission has set itself great ambitions for green hydrogen and also for ammonia. but let's finally bet on the future: sailing boats. yes, the bladder. we know that now, we have a lot of know-how in europe. and for the air we breathe: the obligation for cruise ships to connect when they dock - and i insist, the obligation - or else they will be banned from ports in 2030. i have therefore tabled an amendment to extend this application to all vessels from 2035 onwards. because these giant ships emit as much as 30,000 cars going 20 miles an hour. it is therefore high time to switch to electricity, because it is no longer possible to subject the people to the fumes from these boats. i tell you, europe has everything to be the great leader in the world in decarbonising the maritime sector. time is no longer measured by mere measurements. so let us all take these measures and our responsibilities together, and tomorrow's vote will give a great and beautiful direction to this maritime sector.
mr president, commissioner vălean, dear colleagues, today we make an important step forward for a quick and efficient facilitation for the decarbonisation in the shipping sector. and according to my opinion, i strongly believe that we have managed to propose a piece of legislation that focuses on reducing shipping’s climate impact while encouraging and accelerating investments in that direction. what is of utmost importance is that the report now recognises the structural role of the commercial operators in the shipping sector. needless to say that we focused on this aspect during our internal negotiation, and i would like to thank once again my colleague warborn for his excellent collaboration. the commercial operator, which can be the ship-owner, which can be the charterer or any other entity, is the entity responsible for making the choice on the compliant fuels used by this ship. the current approach now fully reflects the ‘polluter pays’ principle, which was established under the treaty of the functioning of the european union, constitutes the fundamental stone of our eu environmental legislation. therefore, the application of the polluter pays principle shall not be subject of contractual negotiations, but an unquestionable eu environmental principle that must be respected by all relevant stakeholders. we also aim to achieve consistency between this regulation in line with ets and the mrv legislation. this report also reflects to the shared responsibility between the commercial operator and the fuel supplier to a very satisfying degree. an obligation on fuel suppliers is provided to compensate shipping companies for the payment of penalties if they fail to deliver compliant fuels pursuant to a contractual arrangement. it is a balanced approach. it has been secure and i believe that this report should also work as a solid base for the trilogue discussion with the commission and the council. once again, thank you very much all co-rapporteurs and the rapporteur.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, this is a very important regulation. the availability of charging and refuelling infrastructure primarily for cars, light commercial vehicles and trucks, but also for the maritime and aviation sectors is a key parameter in determining the future market share of low and zero emission vehicles in europe in the coming years. the more charging points there are, the more attractive it will be for consumers and operators to switch to cleaner propulsion technologies, such as electric and hydrogen vehicles. we are at a crossroads today: this legislation will set the course for the next decades in the field of mobility, and is an important step towards an energy-independent europe, based on green renewable sources. having an adequate number of efficient and easy charging points is the most correct way to make our will to achieve the objectives of the climate law and repowereu really feasible and tangible. for a clean europe, an energy self-sufficient europe, a stronger europe.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the road to decarbonisation of the maritime sector is still long and uncertain. the marine fuels regulation sets out a realistic and gradual emission reduction trajectory and provides a boost to investment in research and development, while respecting the principle of technological neutrality. some are betting on hydrogen, others on ammonia and methanol, or on biofuels or batteries, without forgetting the role liquefied natural gas can play in a transition phase, ensuring an early and immediate reduction in co2. because no single winning option is on the horizon, all players in the sector will have to contribute, from operators, who are subject to strict obligations, to ports, who will have to install shore-side power infrastructure, to suppliers of sustainable fuels, to the shipyards, which require creativity and innovation. the compromise we are about to vote on represents, in my view, a good balance between the opposing extremes, between those who do not consider social costs and impacts and those who seem to ignore the climate crisis. finally, ladies and gentlemen, let us not forget that this is a challenge which we can only overcome if we are also able to act in international fora. this is an essential element to avoid unfair competition from operators and ports in third countries, while ensuring the decarbonisation of the maritime sector at global level.
mr president, madam commissioner, i would like to thank you for your kind words. citizens need to feel safe when buying new alternative fuel transport, especially if they travel a lot outside their daily routes and familiar area. if they are worried and doubtful about whether they will get their vehicle to their destination on time, they will be less inclined to buy such a vehicle. i therefore support this proposal and the strategy for the timely and adequate construction of alternative fuel charging stations. although there is an increasing number of alternative fuel transport infrastructure available, the question is whether all countries will be able to provide sufficient charging points on their road infrastructure in the foreseeable future. i would also like to mention the planned phase-out of combustion engines. science is also progressing in this area and the suggestions of experts should be taken into account and the continued use of such vehicles should be allowed to continue, at least to a certain percentage.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we are voting this week on two proposals for regulations, both on maritime and infrastructure, which are central to meeting the 2050 climate targets. maritime transport will probably be one of the most demanding sectors in this transition process. conscious of this need, the commission has shown ambition in the texts it has presented to us, supported by impact studies and their proper quantification. nevertheless, parliament, in almost all the 'fit for 55' packages, has repeatedly scaled up the level of ambition to figures which we consider unrealistic. i give as examples the increase in the installed power of charging stations, the distance between them, the increase in the dates for their implementation or the air conditioning system in the cabins of aircraft. in all of them, what i believe is fundamental to our work as co-legislators, the principle of proportionality, has been lacking. however, and since very important commitments have been made in both texts, such as derogations and exemptions for peripheral countries like portugal and also for the outermost regions, something which was not in the commission's initial text, we now welcome and are in a better position to support these proposals.
mr president, commissioner, i want to express my support for these initiatives because the deployment of sufficient, visible and accessible alternative fuel recharging and refuelling infrastructure is an essential condition for the decarbonisation of transport. it obliges industry to innovate in the production of vehicles and fuels. but we also need to provide legal certainty to industry and guarantees to transport professionals and citizens about the charging point network. the deployment of these infrastructures on the ten-t core network must be given priority and simpler and more uniform means of payment than those existing today must also be ensured, especially for electric mobility. the current dispersion is as disincentive as the shortage of recharging points. as regards the regulation on low-emission renewable fuels for the shipping sector, i am again in favour of making the transition with and not against industry. the savings we are told will be made in indirect pollution costs and operational improvements must be applied to innovation. this opens up a huge opportunity for european shipbuilding to consolidate our global leadership in sustainable solutions for this mode of transport. we have an obligation to take advantage of it. finally, i would like to draw attention to the sensitivity we owe to the outermost regions in this matter. they are particularly dependent on shipping. let us give a good reason for the exceptions, but let us also acknowledge the specific nature of these territories.
mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, this regulation provides answers, guidelines and objectives for the air transport sector, for the maritime sector and also for the road sector, particularly for heavy goods transport and for light vehicles. but let us admit that for motorists and in relation to their concerns - in particular that of not having enough terminals - this text does not go far enough: it concerns only the transeuropean network. it will be urgent to provide a rapid response for all areas, for all networks, so that the obligations also help to alleviate the fears of the population. there are many fears. will i be able to afford an electric vehicle? will i be able to afford to run it? will i be able to have enough charging stations near my home? it is a challenge, of course, for citizens, for their jobs and for their work. it is an economic issue, a tourist issue. and so, i think that we will have to add a whole series of obligations very quickly, in particular to ensure that the network is sufficient everywhere on all european roads. i will end by saying that the all-electric as it is decided - i did not vote for this all-electric - is for me a madness, especially of dependence on china. the european strategy and innovation in particular have had a lot to lose with this target set for 2035 of 100%. a target of 90% would have allowed this flexibility for the market, also for new technologies.

mr president, madam commissioner, i would like to congratulate you. your text is moving in the right direction, that of more protection for all critical infrastructure for europe, especially in the health and food sectors. the pandemic has shown us how urgent it is to protect these important sectors, which are vital for europe - health, food, energy - from cyberattacks, among other things. i would urge the commission to go further. i think that as far as our independence is concerned - a form of sovereignty that can be found at european level in such important sectors as energy, digital, food, health and i would even add defence - we must go further to avoid any dependence in the future. it is sometimes created, especially if we take the example of the decision that has been taken here and elsewhere to have 100% electric vehicles by 2035, which creates a form of dependence on china. we also see this when the same china takes over ports and airports in europe. in relation to all these strategic sectors, all these strategic places, the commission must go further to protect europeans, to protect our independence and autonomy to the maximum.
madam president, although some 60% of new university graduates are women, they are underrepresented in many sectors, but especially in the economic sectors and especially in the higher echelons. 31.5% of the members of the boards of directors are women and only 8% of them are chairmen. with this new legislation, which has been a decade in the making, listed companies will have to seek and have to ensure that, by 2026, the underrepresented sex occupies at least 40% of non-executive positions and 33% of executive positions. achieving a balanced representation between all the sexes, between women and men, is an exercise in equality, information, transparency, justice, democracy and peace. greater balance always means better competitiveness. it will drive economic growth, i have no doubt about it: smart, innovative, sustainable and inclusive growth. but it will also improve the competitiveness of businesses and reduce poverty. diversity is synonymous with talent. boosting the presence of women is a key tool for having the best talent.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, equality between men and women is a fundamental principle of european civilization. that is why i am in favour of this directive which calls on the member states of the union to achieve a better balance between the sexes on the boards of large companies. in this respect, the accommodative target of reserving at least 40% of non-executive director positions for the underrepresented sex by 2026 seems reasonable to me. in the short term, of course, it will be women who will benefit from this measure. as iranian women fight for their freedom from the dark regime of the mullahs, i am delighted with the beautiful message of hope we are sending them today. yes, true equality of rights between men and women is possible. no, there are no insurmountable obstacles on the way to it, whether economic, social or religious.
mr. chairman, ladies and gentlemen, if you're very good at your job, and you get promoted and paid more, and someone else does a worse job than you, you'll say it's not fair. yet some politicians in this parliament want to impose precisely such unfair behaviour on businessmen. instead of the quality of the employees being the sole criterion for advancement, this directive arbitrarily imposes quotas on companies, reserved seats, how many women should be, then i guess how many lgbtiq people should be, how many people of different races should be in order for the company not to be labeled racist, and so on. where's the end of this meddling of yours? i'm all for women on the board of directors if they're the best quality employees, but only if they're the best quality employees. so, only quality, nothing arbitrary. the idea that a politician, many of whom have not spent a day in the real sector, knows better than an economist what's good for business is pretty crazy. so let the economy run free.
madam president! despite the changes taking place in the european labour market, despite many ideas to remove the barriers that restrict women's advancement, there are still some stereotypes in our assessment of competence. women still experience the glass ceiling and the parenthood lens that constrains our professional development. many people, including those in this room, agree that legislation that seeks to create equality, to create equal opportunities for women and men, is artificial and counterproductive. i don't agree with that approach, because when you look at women's education and competence, you see that women have made incredible progress. unfortunately, this does not mean that we are using our potential, our abilities, and our skills. it's a loss that affects the economy and society. it's a waste that should not be allowed to continue. the project we're talking about today is all about equality of opportunity, about a fair selection process for the top jobs. it does not favor any group. i'm aware we have a long way to go. it took ten years before we got to this discussion and decision today. but change is necessary and i believe that the implementation of this directive is a good start for change in the broad economic field.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. where the market fails, where companies fail to prove that they can regulate themselves, that is, get themselves under control, we have to, the state has to regulate. you, the companies, have had plenty of time. that's why today's a good day. 40% of the seats on supervisory boards must be held by women, one third of all executive board members. this is a milestone. young women now know that they will not be overlooked in their performance, that performance is worthwhile. and that's the message i want to give to the far right. the fish stinks from the head. the fact that young women are overlooked is because management is all male, and therefore it is the crucial step to overcome male society and reach the human society. thanks to lara wolters, thanks to evelyn regner for their work on this point. but this milestone is a milestone and not the end point: women still hold more than 40% and one third of the management positions. so now companies are getting a second chance to create real equality and to match reality with these rates.
the president, commissioner, colleagues. for almost ten years, the proposal for a european commission directive was blocked by the member states. in those ten years of negotiations, we have unfortunately seen little progress in europe in terms of the presence of women on boards. that is precisely why this directive is so important. from 1 july 2026, the composition of the boards of directors will need to be more balanced. yes, in an ideal world, quotas might be redundant, but we see that they are needed and we see that they work. for example, in belgium, there has been a clear improvement since the introduction of the federal quota law of 2012. in 2020, for the first time, more than one third of the total number of members of the board of directors were women. ten years ago, it was less than 10%. in 2022, boards of directors should no longer be synonymous with closed men's clubs. we need women at every table where important decisions are made. only in this way will we evolve towards a more balanced union, towards a more balanced society.
on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, dear commissioner, dear colleagues, we have all kind of opinions about each other in this parliament, but at the end of the day we are all good people with good intentions and just different ideas. we must remember this when we speak about difficult topics, we must recognise together that until now none of our actions solved the migration crisis. instead, they have allowed more and more people to die in the sea. they have allowed human smugglers to get richer. they have fed false hope and led innocent people to cursed roads. i am ashamed when i see these results. we have abandoned the real refugees. we have given power to human smugglers. we have turned our shores into silent cemeteries of people’s children, mothers or fathers and their dreams. this must change. false compassion and actions that actually lead to a worse situation are not enough. the open borders and the current chaos are a threat to schengen and an invitation to die. the question is not how to save a boat in italy, the question is why could this boat even depart from the first harbour? the question is also why some ngos act like ferries for the human smugglers in the mediterranean sea. we must be clear, we must be firm and we must be fair. we must be strict with illegal migrants with no need of protection and fair to actual victims. only then can we implement true solidarity with migration policy that is aligned with our sense of humanity. to achieve this, we must face reality. we need partnerships with neighbouring countries, even if we sometimes hold our nose. we need to go back to the idea of external processing of asylum requests. we need an information campaign that shows the truth to young people, we need to address the problem of returns, and we need to help italy and greece. we can do this if we leave ideology and face reality, speak frankly with each other.
mr president, the asylum crisis is back from the dead. in belgium there are now 40 000 asylum seekers a year, in the whole of europe there are close to one million. that is not sustainable and i have therefore tabled a resolution, a resolution which seeks to resolve the matter once and for all. the proposal is to repeal the 1967 new york protocol. we would then be taking in refugees from europe only, as we are doing now with the numerous ukrainian refugees. that was the intent of the 1951 refugee convention after world war ii: the european union is a great example of this. but in 1967 that protocol removed that limitation and we are now expected to welcome the whole world here. it has to stop. allowing millions of migrants into our country will disrupt our society and implode our social security system. i therefore ask for your support for this resolution.

member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, i wish to thank this parliament for again having the situation in belarus on the agenda and for your relentless support to the people of belarus. we addressed the situation in belarus and its involvement in russia’s war of aggression against ukraine at the last plenary on 19 october 2022. on that occasion, the high representative, josep borrell, condemned the involvement by belarus in the strongest possible terms and expressed eu support to belarussians facing the worsening repression of lukashenko’s regime. unfortunately, this repression continues to intensify. there are now more than 1450 political prisoners – a number that increases by the day and that includes nobel peace prize winner, ales bialiatski. this is just the tip of the iceberg. since august 2020, almost 40 000 people have been temporarily arrested, including some 500 journalists, of whom 33 remain in prison. political opponents have been sentenced to long prison terms, up to 18 years, and the opposition in exile is criminalised and would face draconian sentences if they were to return to belarus. recent amendments to the criminal code, which can be used retroactively, open the possibility of the use of the death penalty to punish ‘attempted acts of terrorism’. most political activists are potentially affected by these amendments. the eu has led three successful resolutions in the un human rights council to report on the situation in belarus and establish a robust accountability mechanism under the high commissioner for human rights. we support, both politically and financially, the international accountability platform for belarus, which collects evidence of the crimes with a view to holding the perpetrators accountable. the eu has clearly declared that the lukashenko regime lacks any democratic legitimacy, and the eu strongly supports the demand for free and fair elections. in this context, we continue to support the democratic forces and we strongly encourage them to remain united. we will continue to provide strong support to the civil society and to the people of belarus, as we have already done with over eur 100 million in direct support. also, as you know, the eu has already imposed six rounds of sanctions against the belarus regime, and work is currently ongoing to adopt more sanctions in the context of human rights violations, for instance, against judges and prosecutors. in the event of further belarusian involvement in russia’s war against ukraine, further areas will be identified. we also regularly engage with the main leader of the belarussian opposition, ms tsikhanouskaya. she has been received by the eu at the highest levels, including by president of the commission, ursula von der leyen, and by president of the european council, charles michel. most recently, she also had an informal meeting with all eu foreign ministers ahead of the november foreign affairs council. to conclude, our engagement remains strong. we will continue to support the legitimate quest of belarussian people for the rule of law, for free and fair elections, and for independence.

a writer. the commission has already made a number of proposals. where do we get our raw materials? the question is one we ask far too rarely, and far too often the answer is: from a war zone. we source over 70% of our cobalt from the eastern part of the drc, and we store over 60% of our coltan there. these are all substances that are in every single cell phone. and it is there that in recent days almost 200,000 people have had to flee from armed rebel groups such as m23, fdlr, etc. in the congo, nearly 6 million people are now internally displaced. that's the highest rate in the world, but here it's just one of many forgotten conflicts. this can't go on! we have a damn responsibility to these people, just because we get so much of our raw materials from them. that means we have to provide humanitarian aid now. the neighbouring countries must stop and prevent the illegal import of the raw materials of the conflict. but we also need to stop funding these rebel groups in the long term and make sure that the conflict-related material regulation really works. but above all, all the regional parties to the conflict must come together and finally clarify their geo-economic interests once and for all, otherwise war and the displacement of people will never end.

executive vice-president of the commission. – madam president, i’ve listened very carefully to the contributions of the honourable members, and what i sense, in conclusion, is a strong willingness to move forward fast on this issue by many in this plenary. and i welcome that and i hope we can move forward very fast. but having listened also to some other members of this house, i would like just to take the time i have to take us back to why we’re doing this. why did we put repowereu on the table? you know, it’s only about two hours from here by plane where young men and women are dying every day because they’re trying to protect their country from an autocratic invader. and this autocrat has weaponised the resource he has, which is fossil fuels, to try not just to subjugate and conquer ukraine, but also to bring us to our knees and to impose his vision of an autocratic society on the whole of europe. and he still believes that he can use energy to weaken us and to bring us to our knees. and i think this should be in our minds when we discuss the measures we need to take today. we need to rapidly increase europe’s energy sovereignty. we can no longer be dependent on outside energy resources which come with political strings attached. so we need to speed up the transition to renewables. we need to rapidly reduce our energy consumption. we need to diversify our energy resourcing – as much as we still need fossil fuels – and diversify it in such a way that not one external party can use that energy as a political weapon to weaken us or to influence us. that is what repowereu is all about. i wanted to emphasise this because i think we, all of us individually, at least once a day, we have to think about these young men and women who are dying for freedom, who are dying for european values, who are dying to protect their country and to keep that country’s independence. they are fighting for european values. they are fighting for our way of life. and they should get the support also in a way that leads us to be stronger and more sovereign in the energy field so we can rebuild ukraine together with them once this aggression is over.
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. the commission is not prepared to accept this. i am very pleased to see the consensus here in the european parliament on speeding up the approval procedures. i am also pleased that we will be able to make some compromises on the subject of species protection. dear s&d, no one here wants to eliminate environmental laws. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that the community is not able to meet its own needs. the subject of biomass also fits into this picture. the commission has made a mistake: to write 'biomass' deliberately is not to include it. it is clear that this is causing some reflection in the european parliament. i admit, you can't treat it like solar and wind, i know that. but we'll have to think of something. we shall probably have a very close vote here in the european parliament. we have to think about how we can get biomass with accelerated approval procedures. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. because it was a good initiative by the european parliament to take up the council regulation, to take the most important points from it - the good points - and to combine that with repowereu, and to do so in a debate. and we're going to settle this in a vote. we have also made a small contribution to speeding up the procedure. i would like to thank all the colleagues who have made this possible. and a big thank you to the staff who have been fantastic supporters. i look forward to the vote on wednesday. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect.
in writing. the rapid and efficient increase in capacity for clean and renewable energy is crucial to achieving the objectives of the paris agreement. the rapid transition to renewable energy is also a necessary part of europe's decoupling from russian fossil fuels. i am therefore pleased that parliament supports the acceleration and streamlining of the administrative and licensing processes for renewable energy production facilities and the more accelerated process of the 'go-to' areas for renewable energy sources. however, it is also important to take account of the new energy facilities that are being built, which is why it is great that the european parliament has supported the idea that natura 2000 nature reserves or ecological corridors cannot be considered as areas of rapid development of renewable energy. this is important to safeguard biodiversity and ecosystem services. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a regulation on the establishment of a european environment agency. i do not think that the increase in bioenergy capacity should be supported or encouraged by the member states.
in writing. the legislative document we are debating today and voting on tomorrow, adapting the processes for realising the repowereu initiative to the new challenges we are facing in the field of energy transition, is of enormous importance. it is essential to combine a strong response to the emergency and the impacts of war and russian blackmail on the access of european households and businesses to energy with continued union leadership in the fight against climate change. some people want to use this window of opportunity to stop the energy transition, the green deal and decarbonisation. voting on this report, without any last-minute distortions, will be to affirm a strong commitment between speeding up the ability to respond to people's immediate needs and consolidating the principles and objectives of environmental sustainability of the planet. engaging national and regional authorities more and better, enhancing people's participation as enshrined in the energy union governance regulation, speeding up decision-making processes without breaking down transparency and defeating bureaucracy are key steps to living up to our mandate and serving those who elected us to be the voice of their problems and the promoter of the answers they need.

during my speech. for the last 15 years, i was working at an energy company: a company that i founded and was running as ceo; a company that grew from zero to 3 billion in revenue; a company that was the largest energy trader in southern and eastern europe; a company that grew from zero to the largest supplier of electricity in slovenia; and a company that in recent years was also the largest provider of solar power plants, meaning that we did embark on investing in renewables as well. having said that, let's switch to the main topic, and that is energy. it's totally clear to everyone that over-dependence on one gas source, which is from russia, led to the weaponization of that particular energy source during the last year, and that we are facing the consequences of that weaponization right now. but even though the lack of supply was rightly addressed by the swift action of both the parliament and the commission during the last weeks meaning that just by combining the three factors which is being able to reduce demand, being able to find new sources from friendly countries and being able to keep the pipelines open throughout europe by implementing the solidarity clause, we were able to cover for the energy needs it was not enough to address the prices. and this is one topic that i always emphasize whenever i speak to my colleagues at the european council and that is that, yes, a lot has been done, but more needs to be done as well in the future. and this week is crucial in that regard. so either we address properly the high energy prices and when i say properly it means decisively, and when i say decisively, we need to implement regulatory changes in the market mechanisms because the internal energy market at the moment, i will not say it is not working, i will say is being worked by the speculative money, but for their own profits and not for the benefits of the population of europe. and this is where the responsibility of the european council comes into light, because it is up to us to address this issue in the proper manner. and we've already asked the commission once to come up with a set of rules, a set of measures, which will adequately address this issue and i can't say that i'm happy with what the commission has come up with. it won't work. it's not enough. we will need to be more decisive in order to reduce the volatility in the markets. we will need to be more decisive to eliminate price spikes. and we will need to be more decisive when it comes to the gas price caps. the energy ministers are sitting in the council today and i certainly hope and urge that they will come to a common decision by whatever means in order to show the unity not to the population but to the markets, because we need to show the unity to the speculative traders and, remember, i made my living doing that, so i know what i'm talking about. so we need to show them a clear message and unity that we will not be left drained. we will not let them drain our budgets. we will not let them drain the global competitiveness of our industry for their profits. it's a very important message, and that's why i'm repeating it all over the place and i'm doing it right now. but the future doesn't stop today and it doesn't stop this winter, so we need to look forward, forward, and the green transition is actually not just the only way forward, but it's also the only way that addresses the three issues simultaneously. it does improve and it does bring autonomy, independence, energy independence to europe. it is the only way to energy independence. it does mitigate climate change because that's the only way how to reduce the impact of energy on climate. and it also, at the end of the day, brings lower energy prices. so we do address all three issues simultaneously and that means that, again, we cannot linger. we need to move as bold and as ambitious as possible. if that means speeding up the permitting process, well, yes. if that means building more, not just interconnectors, but more gas and pipe and power lines, yes, we do need more power lines at all levels. we can put them underground, but we need them and we need more storage capacity as well to enable the grids to connect more renewables. and this is really the only way forward. and if we act boldly and decisively and if we link the wind potential of the north with some potential of the south put some hydro in the mix and some storage in between yeah, in the next 10 to 15 years, we could be totally renewable in europe. it is a vision that is doable. okay, it might take us five more years than that, but it's doable. and that's the most important message. and for that reason, not just because of the energy crisis that we're facing now, we really owe it, we owe it to our children to do it now because it's doable so there's absolutely no excuse that we wait with our next steps when it comes to that. before entering politics, while i was still the executive, i was also the eu climate pact ambassador. i was promoting, and sincerely promoting it, because for living i was doing energy trading but for my soul i was promoting the self-sustainable home energy systems the systems where each of us that owns a home can become totally independent on himself, on him, on hers or his energy needs, for all energy needs. electric vehicle, sunroof, solar heating, heat pumps and storage by doing that, we helped thousands and thousands of households in slovenia to become totally independent from the existing crisis. so they're not just helping the environment, at the moment they're totally independent on the energy crisis. the one thing that we're addressing all the time when it comes to the energy transition, we're always addressing the how do we deal with the energy for our machines, for our technologies. but we are overlooking one important thing. this is my second message. we're overlooking the how do we address the energy that we need to fuel our bodies, which is food. the food system that we are utilising right now is totally unsustainable for our future, totally. unless we change the food production and food consumption in a very thorough way, we will not be able to meet any of the climate goals. because if the rest of the world were to follow in our footsteps, we would need seven planets just to sustain the food consumption, which is obviously not something that can be done. now, of course, each of us can start a change in himself or herself. changing the diet. of course, that's easy. just switch from less meat i'm not saying no meat but that s consume less meat, let s consume more plant-based food. anyway, it's healthier, but this is not the story today. but in that way, we will help the planet and we will help our children as well. not just by teaching them how to be healthier, but also in teaching them how to be sustainable. but that's an individual choice. what is the responsibility of the government or of the authorities? the main responsibility is the building of the awareness, scientific facts when it comes to food plus and this is important to enable the choice, enable the choice of healthier food to be available in public systems. that's what we did on a small scale in slovenia back home. and results are amazing. we didn't have to force anyone and 50% of consumers at a particular facility switched within a month on a totally plant-based food. it was an individual choice that was enabled by the authorities. not enforced this is very important. enforced. and that is the responsibility of all of us who are in the political system. by saying that, the first thing that comes to your mind once you have to make a decision on whether you step into on behalf of the band the left. madam president, mr prime minister, welcome to a ceo, a business owner who becomes prime minister directly. that's obviously something! i shall therefore deal with an element which was not mentioned in your speech, namely the question of social dumping. mr president, do you know the concept of social dumping? the commission has also proposed that the member states should be able to provide for the possibility of a national system of compensation for the loss of earnings resulting from the transfer of workers to another country. the framework for freedom of movement within the european union is called the 'freedom of movement for services'. and this social dumping today is due in particular to differences in social contributions and gross wages. in this case, social contributions are paid in the country where the contract was signed, rather than in the country where the work is performed. for companies this is often interesting, because they get a lot more to win and they pay more social security contributions. the workers are given a drama. the borealis scandal in antwerp shows that it is also becoming increasingly commonplace for non-european workers, workers who are being exploited shamelessly, and who are being sent to portugal via hungary to be tried in antwerp, belgium. there is social dumping, as you know, in various sectors: transport, construction... way too many sectors. in your country there is often a major role to play, so that even a european trade union federation complains about slovenia. this may be done under pressure from larger countries or other countries which have been urged by the slovenian government to do so. i looked at the numbers. almost one third of construction workers in slovenia are sent abroad, the highest percentage in the european union. six out of ten posted workers from slovenia are nationals of non-european countries, or at least non-members of the european union. in other words, companies established in slovenia are looking for workers abroad, not because they need them, but to send them immediately to other member states, exploit them to the full and make more profit, simply and, by the way, destroy labour law. this system exempts an amount of €128 million from social contributions. it's a real social security hold-up, let's be honest, and we're still talking about one country. i was in your country, in slovenia, about a year ago, under the previous government. i had a meeting with a slovene worker in the office, and she told me something that really shocked me. in a building of a ministry the same building as a ministry i had a list of postbus companies. the commission has also adopted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. so in the building of a ministry! so, mr prime minister, social dumping, whether through secondment or subcontracting, is a problem in europe today. we know there are solutions. this means, in particular, that the free movement of services must be subject to guarantees of working conditions, the principle of equal work for equal pay, the rejection of any discrimination between employees on the basis of the place where the contract was signed, and, i think, the drawing up of a european directive on the limitation of subcontracting chains, which serve only social dumping. so a question, mr prime minister: what will your government do to change the fundamental role played by slovenia in social dumping?

president, commissioner, minister, colleagues, poverty and social exclusion still affect 91 million europeans, including 18 million children. the coronavirus crisis and the war in ukraine have only made things worse. rising prices are making it difficult for people in poverty to survive. the european child guarantee aims to ensure that every child has access to essential basic services: the european union is a major player in the world economy. only in this way can we break the cycle of generational poverty. however, there are still member states which have not submitted a national action plan, and this is incomprehensible. however, the esf+ provides financial resources to support those actions. and yes, it is important to monitor the implementation of these very closely in order to have a clear view of the use of resources. but exceptional circumstances call for exceptional measures and if additional financial resources are needed, commissioner, then we must provide them. because if you don't invest in children in poverty, you lose a whole generation. we have set ourselves targets to lift five million children out of poverty by 2030. we have to make this happen.

president, commissioner, with the decision we have taken today to end the vice-presidency of eva kaili, we have sent a strong signal. europe is not for sale, never. that was also a necessary signal, because this corruption scandal touches the heart of our european parliament, of our european project. everything about qatar - and i think everyone has said it here - has to be back on the table, starting with visa-free travel. we must give full cooperation to the judicial inquiry, in which the belgian services have done an excellent job. but this is just the beginning. we need to be fully transparent about all lobbying contacts in and around the european parliament and look at how the other european institutions deal with them. and it's wider than that. we must strictly follow the recommendations of the parliamentary committee on foreign interference. we're a target, we need to realize that. this calls for a coordinated european approach and also a genuine committee of inquiry. ladies and gentlemen, interventions must be based on vision, on conviction, and not on money in your bank account. let this be a turning point.
mr. chairman, please! ladies and gentlemen! today, garcía pérez speaks about defending the honour of the socialists and democrats after the arrest of an influential s&d member. for years, it was the s&d that brutally trampled on the honor and dignity of poland, without regard for the facts and circumstances. so today many people in poland are asking, were these unjustified attacks on poland sponsored? the corruption scandal in the european parliament exposes hypocrisy and double standards which cannot be tolerated. it is also revealing double standards in the commission, and the european commission must finally address this problem. it is therefore necessary to set up a committee composed of representatives of the member states to examine the functioning of the union institutions. it is necessary to pay particular attention to cases of high-level lobbying and corruption not only by qatar but also by russia. in cases such as the nord stream gas pipeline or climate policy, it is necessary to increase member states' control over the eu institutions.
mr president, madam commissioner, a million and a half euros recovered and many offices searched, sealed up, in this parliament is probably the tip of the iceberg. the problem is structural. i remember that whenever we proposed to reduce the income of members of parliament, you replied: 'no, members of parliament must be paid, otherwise they will be corrupted.' you've been singing that to me for years, and clearly, it hasn't worked. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. but it is also a serious matter that members of parliament are legally influenced by lobbies, multinationals, revolving doors and board seats. the problem is that it is a problem of money culture. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: the council of ministers is not a political body. most people prefer to flirt with the bourgeoisie and live like ordinary people. it is high time that politicians learn to serve the people instead of their own interests of multinationals.
president. - mr president, i am as shocked as you are by the events. at the same time, i am happy that we have a high-performing police force and an independent judiciary. and we certainly need to tighten up our own rules on transparency and accountability. but i would like to warn against an interinstitutional ethics body, a body made up of third parties - even if they are independent - that would monitor our compliance with our rules. we must not allow ourselves to be co-controlled by a body that has been co-appointed by the executive. a parliament that respects itself controls the executive, not the other way around. we must instead work to strengthen our own committee which enforces our own code of conduct.
mr president, dear colleagues, this weekend, a 16—year—old girl was sitting at home in jenin, palestine. when she heard people scream outside, she went to the roof to see what was going on. and on the rooftop of her own house an israeli soldier shot and killed her; two bullets to the face, one to the neck, one to the shoulder. jana zakarneh, an innocent child, another one killed. this year, israeli forces have assassinated several tens of palestinian children, continue to destroy palestinian schools and homes, and steal, occupy ever more land. but what does the european union do? nothing! worse, it intensifies relations with israel. you speak of human rights, but you let israel kill with impunity. that hypocrisy disgusts me!
i have listened to you very carefully. i also read your opinion yesterday in the flemish newspaper de tijd, among others. good luck with the task force that's been formed. i hope that you will really succeed in eliminating discrimination against european companies. but we do have european homework. agreed state aid to the extent that it is aimed at innovation, renewable energy, excellence or market failure. but your temporary crisis framework is creating very big problems, it's upsetting the level playing field. in the same newspaper, de tijd, you can read the cry of distress from large flemish companies, such as unilin, kronos, aluminium duffel. they cannot compete with large companies from large member states which enjoy massive state aid and are threatened with closure. trust in the eu is thus diminished. fix it quickly.
in writing. i would like to ask you, ladies and gentlemen, to stop being so naive about the united states of america, which you generally trust blindly. because at a time when war is raging on the european continent, the united states not only takes advantage of this to sell us their lng cargoes at absolutely ridiculous prices, with up to $200 million in profits per cargo, but in addition... they pass a law that clearly aims to weaken the european economy. this is not the behavior of a true ally. so what do we do? liberal solutions are "has been". they're out of date. who still thinks that by flooding the private sector with money, we're going to get away with it? faced with the failure of european liberalisation, which has failed to guarantee low energy prices, failed to guarantee the social climate transition, failed to guarantee employment, it is time to change the rules of the game, to ensure that the profit of the big multinationals is no longer the guiding principle of european political decisions. let's accept that some sectors are too important to be left in the hands of the market. let us publicly take up strategic sectors such as energy, transport, pharmaceuticals, digital, etc. let's switch.
the reason for inflation and falling purchasing power is the skyrocketing price of energy. this is the case, for example, in france, where nuclear power plants are being divested, whereas that country used to obtain 75% of its electricity from nuclear energy. in france, where nuclear power once accounted for three quarters of the country's electricity supply, there are even warnings of power cuts. or even more stupidly, in belgium, where the governments - including the n-va - have consistently confirmed the nuclear phase-out, so that we now have to burn expensive gas to replace the cheap electricity from our nuclear power stations. how crazy can you think of it. moreover, it will mean that we will soon have too little gas to supply our homes and our industry. of course the other parties here claim that the war in ukraine is the cause, but to my knowledge the russians have not decided on the nuclear step. you did. the result of all this is that we now have to beg for expensive lpg in qatar from the friends of the socialists. - that's great.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the economist - your magazine, the liberal and capitalist magazine of the european union and europe, which you all read - has calculated that up to 185 000 more people could die of cold this winter, not only because of the cold but also because of your inaction. your inaction on the energy price freeze: we have been asking for a gas price freeze for a year. how do you expect people to trust the european union, if they have to wait a year and potentially die at home because they can't pay the bill? your inaction on the excess profits of multinationals: ah, taxing your friends multinationals, it's always too complicated, even when they make excess profits and even when people can't get along anymore. but aren't you ashamed to let this happen? don't be surprised when people take to the streets, strike and mobilize to put you in front of your responsibilities. because that's what you have to do today, put pressure on yourself, otherwise you'll never listen.
for the greens/efa group mr president, reporters without borders denounces the bleak daily life of many journalists in europe: physical attacks, freedom-threatening laws, harassment, intimidation, censorship, lack of pluralism and independence, concentration of the media and surveillance. press freedom is under threat in 22 of the 27 member states, according to the european commission. these are threats to the freedom of the press and its work, which provoke intolerable forms of self-censorship, while journalists often denounce corruption, violations of the rule of law and injustices committed by the powerful and european leaders. daphne caruana galizia in malta, ján kuciak in slovakia, giórgos karaïváz in greece, peter de vries in the netherlands: these investigative reporters died for their work, murdered on european soil. others, who have revealed the illegal use of spyware such as pegasus by our governments and secret services, often against themselves, are now being prosecuted and intimidated in our states. julian assange, who was a finalist for the european parliament's sakharov prize, is currently facing 175 years in prison in the us for his work as a whistleblower. what happened to the sacred freedom of the press? the media, along with the judiciary, are vital counter-powers to our democracies and our rule of law. the qatargate scandal, which is shaking our institution, is a reminder that some people are allowing themselves to be corrupted at the expense of democracy and that foreign interference in european affairs is also aimed at undermining our democracies. this scandal was revealed by the existence of counter-powers. in the face of an increasingly muzzled press, we will not remain silent, and we recall the teaching of montesquieu: 'in order to prevent abuse of power, power must stop power by the order of things.' let us protect journalists, let us protect what is most precious to our democracies, and let us punish those who allow themselves to spy on these counter-powers, and those who prevent a free and independent press from functioning!
the european parliament has also called for the eu to be given the right to take action in the fight against terrorism. corruption, for example. daphne caruana galizia was killed in malta by a bomb planted in her car. because of crime. dutch crime reporter peter r. de vries was shot dead on the streets of amsterdam. because of islamism and too much tolerance for intolerance. the cartoonists of charlie hebdo were killed in one fell swoop at their editorial meeting in paris. more and more journalists are being forced to go into hiding when they write a piece about islam. it starts with censorship, intimidation and threats, and often ends with violence or even murder. but the threat to our rule of law is not just from outside. it also comes from within and is tempted, for example, to stop thinking, to let prejudice and the pensée unique take over. journalists must face the facts and never allow themselves to be dominated by militancy. otherwise it becomes activism and it's not journalism anymore. then our fellow citizens will increasingly distrust and turn away from the traditional media and become more susceptible to disinformation and populism. that, too, undermines our rule of law. the press must therefore urgently be self-critical in order to reconnect with our fellow citizens. no cancellation culture, no wokism, no self-censorship out of fear, but freedom. the duty to inform. freedom of thought. that was the motto of the newspaper of my mentor, investigative journalist norbert zongo, who was brutally murdered in burkina faso in 1998 for reporting on corruption. i still feel anger and sadness when i think about it, but i'm also proud of him. he was the one and only fourth pillar of a dying democracy. without a free press, there is no democracy. we need not only the separation of powers, which montesquieu spoke of, but also the strength of the fourth power. this fourth power: a critical and independent press.
